How to Style White Jeans for Fall

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These outfits prove white denim has no off-season

White jeans are now a year-round staple, no longer relegated to the summer months between Memorial Day and Labor Day. From work-ready looks anchored by tailored denim to relaxed ensembles made for weekend getaways, these white-denim outfits do it all. This fall, take inspiration from a few of our favorite street-style looks. The lineup includes everything from straight-leg and relaxed fits to low-rise baggy silhouettes, in a range of soft hues like off-white and ecru. When paired with capsule wardrobe classics, such as a white T-shirt, a brightly colored button-down shirt, or a barn jacket, the results are stylish and equally easy to pull off.

Below, discover ten white jeans outfits that prove how cool they can look throughout the fall/winter months.

There’s a reason winter whites are a perennial favorite. Matching white jeans with your outerwear creates a clean, fresh monochromatic look. For a subtle sliver of color, layer an icy blue cardigan underneath, then add classic black pumps, and finish with sculptural gold earrings.

A simple way to wear white jeans during the fall is pairing them with a ladylike jacket in brown check print. The cropped silhouette offers a flattering counterpoint to relaxed denim, while black accessories—a roomy shoulder bag and ballet flats—lend a refined touch. Consider this an easy fall workwear formula you can wear on repeat.

If you’re a minimalist, try playing with contrasting textures to make your white jeans feel even more seasonally appropriate. Style them with a roomy black suede funnel-neck jacket, pointed-toe boots, and an unexpected accessory, like this elegant tassel bag.

The best fall outfits start with great basics: White straight-leg jeans, a white T-shirt, and a lightweight trench, which are all part of a well-rounded capsule wardrobe. Give these classics a hint of surprise by tying a pop of red knit around your shoulders and slipping into mules for an unexpected detail. They’re ideal for peak fall days, when it’s not too hot or too cold, and you can get away with a little peep-toe.

A striped long-sleeve T-shirt lends a touch of nautical charm to white jeans. Go for an oversized option in red to play up the relaxed, off-duty vibe. Black shoes—whether boots, loafers, or sleek sneakers—anchor the look, while a leather bucket bag and aviator frames complete the outfit.

Barn jackets remain a major trend for fall 2026. Make a case for wearing pastels well into the cooler months with a pale pink version and white jeans, offset by a black sweater, loafers, and a brown suede bag. Whether for a casual day spent running errands in the city or a weekend away, this combination strikes the perfect balance.

White jeans don’t always have to be worn in casual settings only. Make yours feel more elegant with a tuxedo-style blazer, silk camisole, and smoking slippers. A structured top-handle bag adds a polished finishing touch, taking the look effortlessly from desk to dinner.

Dip your toes into the season’s brightest color trends by putting a bright button-down shirt with otherwise simple styling pieces, such as a white T-shirt and straight-leg jeans. A leather belt and high-vamp shoes provide pared-back elegance.

French girls know how to layer the je ne sais quoi way, and denim is often the base. White jeans get a Parisian twist when paired with not one but three fall jackets—after all, temperatures are always unpredictable. A chore coat, plaid blazer, and windbreaker jacket can all be part of your layering essentials.

Nothing is more classically cool than wearing black and white during fall/winter. Bring your baggy white jeans into the winter months with a black turtleneck cardigan and sleek textural accessories—an embossed belt, suede flats, and a rich leather bag. Use this look as a base, then add a leather jacket or a tailored wool coat for extra warmth when needed.

Fair Warning to Auction Major Yoshitomo Nara Painting for Up to $15 M.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

One of Yoshitomo Nara’s largest paintings is also one of his most subtle. Instead of glaring defiantly at the viewer, the child at the center of In the Milky Lake / Thinking One (2011) has her eyes closed and a somewhat serene expression on her face. Her body is half-submerged in a pale pool of water. On November 19, the monumental painting will be offered by Loïc Gouzer’s Fair Warning app with an estimate of $10 million to $15 million.

Measuring 8.5 feet by 6 feet, it’s the largest Nara painting ever to appear at auction. The work has been in private hands for 14 years and will be exhibited in Chelsea at 534 West 22nd Street, which has been designed to resemble a Japanese temple, from November 6–19. Related Articles Jeff Koons Appears to Be the 'Distinguished Collector' Offering $100 M. in Old and Modern Masters at Christie's Fair Warning Heads Underground With Keith Haring Subway Drawing Estimated at Just Under $1 M.

Nara painted the work in 2011, the year the Tōhoku earthquake and tsunami devastated northeastern Japan, including the region where he grew up. The disaster left him unable to paint for some time, and when he returned to his studio, something had changed. His familiar cast of angry, sometimes menacing children began to give way to quieter, more introspective figures.

In the Milky Lake / Thinking One was the only major painting he completed that year. It’s since appeared in numerous museum exhibitions, including his 2012 survey in Japan, his 2021 retrospective at the Los Angeles County Museum of Art, and more recent shows at the Guggenheim Bilbao, Museum Frieder Burda, and Hayward Gallery in London. Nara also included it in his 2015 book Self-Selected Works: Paintings.

The sale will take place alongside the November New York auctions from the Christie’s, Sotheby’s, and Phillips. Many of the big-ticket lots being offered are by historical, blue-chip artists whose careers were firmly established in the 20th century, from Kandinsky and Monet to Warhol and Basquiat, as opposed to living contemporary artists. Gouzer, who founded Fair Warning after leaving Christie’s, thinks collectors may be getting ready to look at contemporary art a little differently.

“There was a lot of not very interesting contemporary [art] that was pushed hard and then crashed, deservedly,” Gouzer told ARTnews, referring to the speculative buying that swept through the market between 2021 and 2023.

“The main dogma of the last year and a half, two years, was like, ‘Contemporary is over. It’s only about blue chip,’” he said.

Gouzer isn’t buying it. Nara, he argued, is “as blue chip as it gets for contemporary art,” and his relatively small output sets him apart from some of the younger artists whose markets have taken a beating in recent years. In many cases, collectors paid a premium for work they thought was scarce, only to discover that there was plenty more where it came from.

“Now we’re in a period where people don’t want what their neighbors have,” Gouzer said. “They don’t want something that is like some kind of commodity. Nara is still here and the market is strong, partly because his art is great and also because it’s really rare.”

Nara’s auction record stands at $24.9 million, set at Sotheby’s Hong Kong in 2019 for Knife Behind Back (2000). His market has cooled somewhat since its peak, although major paintings can still fetch eight figures. A similar work, In the Milky Lake (2012), which shares the present painting’s contemplative imagery, sold for $12.8 million at Sotheby’s Hong Kong in 2023.

There’s also been a big change in Nara’s gallery representation. In November 2025, David Zwirner announced that it would represent the artist, who had been with Pace Gallery for 14 years. Pace said it would continue working with him. On November 5, Zwirner will open Nara’s first solo exhibition with the gallery, spread across two Chelsea locations, with new paintings, drawings, sculptures, and earlier works chosen by the artist.

Gouzer thinks the gallery change could inject Nara’s market with new interest. “An artist is a musical note, and then the gallery is an instrument,” he said, noting that three monumental Nara sculptures also recently went on view at the Hirshhorn Museum and Sculpture Garden in Washington, D.C. “So if you change the instrument, you might perceive the note a bit differently.”

Gouzer is confident that Nara’s auction record will eventually fall. He said he wouldn’t be surprised to see a major painting sell for $30 million or even $40 million someday, though he’s not necessarily expecting that to happen this month.

The top goalscorers for club & country in 2026 so far: Kane, Yamal, Mbappe, Raphinha…

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Big names from Real Madrid, Barcelona, Arsenal and Bayern Munich feature alongside some surprise names in the most prolific goalscorers of the calendar year so far.

Some big names are surprisingly absent from the most prolific goalscorers of 2026 to date.

Drawn from Europe’s five major leagues, here are the top 10 goalscorers in 2026 across club and international competitions.

The striker infamously failed to have a touch of the ball in his first 30 minutes of World Cup action this summer, but he put that slow start behind him.

The man who delivered La Roja’s match-winner against England in the Euro 2024 final, he’s in the form of his career. He scored in Real Sociedad’s Copa del Rey final victory over Atletico Madrid and his year went on to get a lot better than that.

He scored five goals in the World Cup, briefly putting himself in dark horse contention for the Golden Boot, but he was never really challenging the superstars at the top of the list.

Another match-winner against England was a sweet bonus for one of the most underrated strikers around.

The jury is still out on Gyokeres, who remains a perplexing forward.

This calendar year has seen him look borderline anonymous and increasingly sidelined at Arsenal.

And yet Gyokeres also fired Sweden to the World Cup with a hat-trick against Ukraine and a late winner against Poland. He’s basically the Ikea Gerd Muller at this point.

The Real Madrid forward was distinctly underwhelming for over a year after vowing “I’ll do it 10x if I have to. They’re not ready” following his Ballon d’Or snub back in 2024.

He’s taken his sweet time, but he’s finally starting to make good on that promise – five of his 24 goals this calendar year were clutch goals in the Champions League, with another two in a big comeback victory over Atletico Madrid.

Up until recently, his overall record for Brazil has left a lot to be desired. Neymar, he ain’t. But his goals against Haiti, Morocco and Scotland in the group stage probably placed him as Brazil’s standout player in an otherwise desperately disappointing World Cup campaign under Carlo Ancelotti.

Things haven’t exactly been going to plan for him this season, though.

Dembele has scored 20 goals in 36 PSG appearances this year, including several crucial strikes in Europe – none bigger than the equalising spot kick he coolly dispatched against Arsenal in the Champions League final.

His goal in France’s World Cup win over Iraq was, incredibly, his first in major tournament football. That was swiftly followed up with a rapid hat-trick against Norway, before another in the quarter-final against Morocco.

He is largely taking it easy right now, once again frequently rested during PSG’s domestic campaign. But he popped up with a goal against Manchester City.

A recent entrant into the top 10 after heading into the international break on the back of a hat trick against Union Berlin.

Olise was the Bundesliga’s best assister last season but has been more than capable of finding the net himself a fair few times too.

He didn’t score any goals at the World Cup but netted the winner in Zinedine Zidane’s second game in charge of France. The best Fantasy Footballer around right now.

Yamal has long been talked up as a Ballon d’Or contender, and slowly but surely he’s getting the numbers to back it up.

The teenager’s goalscoring output has grown season on season, and 2025-26 was no exception. February’s hat-trick against Villarreal was evidence of a player about to go truly stratospheric.

Unfortunately, the final furlong of the campaign was hit by a hamstring injury, or else we suspect he’d be a few places higher up in this list.

He needed just 10 minutes in his first World Cup start to find the back of the net, although that would be his only goal in Spain’s triumph out in North America.

Now he’s looking fit and hungry – at least based on the evidence of his exceptional form of late.

The Brazilian has an exceptional tally of 34 goals for Barcelona so far this calendar year, which is especially impressive when you consider he’s missed quite a lot of action due to injury.

He averages a goal every 51 minutes for Barca this season – to put that in context, Kylian Mbappe averages a goal every 83 minutes for Real Madrid.

Raphinha has scored in all but two of the Barca games he’s played in in 2026-27, scoring hat tricks in two, and currently leads the Europe-wide goalscoring charts with 15 in the league.

The top three are the last three winners of the European Golden Shoe. This list has taken a while to take shape, but the frontrunners are the usual suspects.

Haaland’s four goals against Iraq and Senegal in the group stages finally saw him climb up into the top 10, from which he was surprisingly absent in the latter half of the 2025-26 club season, before he added to his World Cup tally against the Ivory Coast. Then there were two goals against Brazil.

He finally drew a blank as Norway exited the World Cup at the hands of England at the quarter-final stage. He earned a well-deserved rest before spearheading Man City’s new era under Enzo Maresca.

Same as it ever was with nine goals in eight Premier League appearances for City this season, plus braces against Porto and PSG.

Like his rival Haaland, it somehow took Mbappe until mid-June to enter the top 10.

Mbappe kicked off the year in superb style, scoring in six successive starts for Real Madrid (nine goals in total) before dipping considerably with just one goal in his next seven appearances.

By the nature of his 10 goals at the World Cup, we don’t expect to see the Real Madrid superstar dropping back out. His Champions League record is pretty sensational.

Arguably the finest player in world football right now. How often have you been able to say that about an Englishman?

The only player in Europe’s five major leagues to average a better-than-goal-a-game output so far this calendar year, the England captain has 37 goals in 33 appearances for Bayern Munich in all competitions in 2026 – including home and away against Real Madrid and PSG.

He capped off his superb season with a hat-trick in the DFB Pokal final. Two goals against Croatia showed Kane carried his form into the World Cup, where he eventually scored six times.

Unbelievably, the Norway game was Kane’s first knockout appearance for club and country in 2025-26 in which he didn’t score. Which, of course, he followed with a blank against Argentina.

Surprisingly enough, Kane failed to add to his tally by drawing a blank in his first three Bayern appearances of the 2026-27 campaign.

But it’s back to normal service with his Bayern tally standing at 10 goals in 12 games. The Czechs and Croatia have been on the receiving end in the Nations League too.

Still some way to go before catching Lionel Messi’s calendar year record, mind.

Affordability: Real Solutions for a Real Problem

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

For buyers, especially first-time buyers, today’s housing affordability crisis (driven by a host of market challenges, from rising mortgage rates to low inventory and lingering inflation) creates many barriers to entry for homeownership. Is there anything agents can actually do at the local level to move the needle and help their clients achieve their homeownership goals?

Four brokerage leaders recently gathered at RISMedia 38th Annual CEO & Leadership Exchange on a panel titled, “Affordability: Real Solutions for a Real Problem,” to say yes and share their tactics and long-term solutions for buyers facing today’s housing affordability problem.

The session, moderated by RISMedia Founder and CEO John Featherston, included panelists Keith Smith, co-owner, Yes Realty Partners, a Keller Williams Alliance Partner; Ennis Antoine, owner and CEO, EA Realty of Georgia; Katey McGrath, vice president, director of operations, Elfant Wissahickon Realtors®; and Ken Baris, CEO, Berkshire Hathaway HomeServices Jordan Baris Realty.

Here’s a glimpse into some of the questions and answers that provided attendees with some detailed takeaways to help their clients overcome the affordability challenge.

John Featherston: To set the stage, tell us why brokers should care about the housing affordability issue?

Keith Smith: I’m just going to kind of say the awkward thing to the room. Every single one of us makes a living either directly or indirectly from helping people buy and sell. And if we can’t help people buy and sell, we’re not going to be able to put our key in the door. We’re not going to be able to begin our business. Right now, first-time homebuyers can’t buy, and they’re 40? You have Boomers who will not be able to move on. So if we can’t help people do that, this isn’t just a certain entry point. It’s all the way across the board. I think maybe a little bit later we’ll talk about some possible solutions.

Katey McGrath: Obviously it’s good business. And what’s interesting too is at least in our market, the competition for that business is a lot less intense because it is a little bit more work and it’s not as dollar-productive. It’s absolutely more rewarding and more valuable to your communities, but not everybody gets up and says, “I want to sell lower-priced houses.” And so I love new agents getting into the business and focusing on affordability. We love training them up to pre-qualify renters because most of the time the renters don’t know they can buy a house. So it’s a great way to start your real estate business.

And in our company, we have a lot of things that sort of reinforce that culture of homeownership as the most impactful thing you can do in a community. We also create programs in-house that help leverage gift funds to just get people across the line, which I’ll talk about more, but we thread it through the entire “gestalt” of our organization.

John Featherston: What are some of the ground-level tactics that you’re using in your organization?

Katey McGrath: One of the things we do is we train up. If you get a call for a rental, find out if they are capable of qualifying for homeownership. We lean on our lenders. I rely on them and have a high level of expectation that they’re going to understand the down payment programs and the grant programs and the ability to stack them. We have a program called Building Equity where we crowdfund it. The partners of the company seeded it and now every agent in my company gives $5 to $50 every time they sell a house. We have a nice little fund, and we make a gift to anyone who buys a house for $250,000 or less in the city of Philadelphia. The only question we ask is, are you going to move into it?

We find that we can leverage that. If our lenders want to participate in the program and be on our website, they have to match that gift. We just want to keep the level of homeownership in Philadelphia as high as possible. And we also want people to consider other neighborhoods, other house sizes and conditions that maybe the people saving up for their dream homes are not looking at.

John Featherston: Ennis, how do you educate agents to help them find homes their buyers afford?

Ennis Antoine: When talking to my agents, I have to remind them first, you have to learn to stack resources. And what that means is, get involved in the city, the county and third-party companies like Georgia Dreams and ask them, “What programs do you have available to assist with down payment assistance?” In Atlanta, we have a program where the mayor wants to have 20,000 affordable homes built by 2030, so he has instructed his people to have this agency set aside funds to help buyers with down payment assistance; you have to live in our community for five years and it’s forgivable where you don’t have to pay back those funds. The goal is educating your agents on what funds are available and then once they’re educated, educate the buyers on what to expect and to set realistic expectations.

John Featherston: Are you seeing or anticipating any improvement in affordability issues from the ROAD to Housing Act?

Ennis Antoine: I chaired the FPC advisory committee (Federal Political Coordinators under NAR), and we worked on that bill for two years and got it passed. Now here’s the key, though. It is only an incentive. It’s going to take each one of us to inform our city councilmen and mayors on what’s available, and to pass these bills to say, “Hey, listen, how can we incentivize people to buy a home?” And then second, when it comes to housing supply, that’s going to take a while because we have an inventory shortage. It’s not going to happen overnight, but we have to be patient because it’s only a framework on what could happen if we partner with our city council and Realtor® organizations.

John Featherston: Ken, why is it critical to approach the affordability issue as far as you can see? And is it relevant to the specific markets that you’re now serving?

Ken Baris: Affordability is relative. Somebody who says “I can’t afford” is maybe a kid who grew up in a town, can’t afford to be in the town now, but they may be able to afford in another community. It’s being able to understand the benefits people can get. So for instance, Bank of America’s community reinvestment program with a grant of $17,500. We have a mortgage partnership, and we make sure anyone who will qualify for that is going to get that grant and use Bank of America because it’s good sense for the clients.

And if you are really astute about the business, you have people who are tenants—they don’t know they can afford to purchase. You can train people to purchase. You can help people improve their credit: Higher credit score, lower interest rate. You can train people to do things like say, “I will fall in like with four properties. I will not fall in love with one.” Because if you fall in like with four and we make four lower offers, we actually can turn you into a buyer and something more affordable by being willing to present offers. So we inspire a team to do the work and take the time and good things happen for clients.

We use foyersavings.com. A very interesting website; it has three things. Number one, it gives you an ability to save money at an okay interest rate or a good interest rate so that your money builds. Number two, it gives a modest down payment assistance with $600 credit of the first $10,000 you have, you get 6% additional in interest back. That’s not huge, but it’s something.

And three, it shows programs from all over—banks, lenders, community program—that provide grants. You may not know all the programs in your own market that are available. Foyer goes after these programs and makes them available to people that use the site. So it’s just being aware of these things, using your method of communication to get the information out, inspiring people to understand what they can do. If you don’t buy somewhere, you’re going to make somebody else wealthier.

John Featherston: A follow up to that, because you’re going into a lot of the nuances that help new buyers enter the marketplace. Have you made a concerted effort within your firm to make this type of training and education a competitive differentiator in your organization?

Ken Baris: Hugely. I find units to be sexy, not just high prices. And if you have salespeople that are in more affordable communities and they’re trained for these programs, they do better. Another thing that we’ll do is we’ll send out emails of successful buyers. “John thought he was going to rent. We let him know that we thought he could purchase, and the good news for John is we were right. You might be in the same position. Let’s get together.” And that’s the kind of communication we’re doing. I can’t change somebody’s income, but I could help them learn how to save more, how to improve their credit and how to get down payment assistance. $17,500 can make somebody from not a buyer to a buyer. And it’s a gift. It’s not a loan.

John Featherston: How are you and your agents getting past the current rhetoric that we hear among young people when they say they’re never going to be able to buy a home?

Katey McGrath: One of the biggest myths is that you think you need 20% down. And so it’s education, education, education, myth busting, destigmatizing these programs. It’s super important for young people in your community, but also make sure that your agents feel that way. And it’s great because like I said, the younger agents eat it up and it keeps them busy early in their career.

Ennis Antoine: I put pen to paper. So as far as paying $2,000 a month in rent, I’m saying, let’s talk about this for a second. A landlord just received $24,000 from you for one year. You say I’m staying there for three or four years. Then you have nothing to show for it. Whereas if you bought a home or a condominium, you pay this amount each month, you would have a tax write off and within four or five years, you’ll see a 4% or 5% increase in the value of the property. You can sell that property, walk away with cash, get the tax write off on your tax returns. And so if it was me, I would buy and invest and start to build wealth.

And if you build wealth, you can get a larger home and pay less money. So the key here is you’re a smart person. Let’s make a smart decision. Let’s buy.

Keith Smith: I want to pivot a little bit. We’re talking about how we can make a difference. Just last year through a land trust we put together 40 permanent affordable new construction units. I’m in Charlottesville with the second-highest cost of living in the state of Virginia, the second-highest cost of housing in Virginia. But we were able to do 40 of them. And the way we did this is what I call the four pillars: The local government, the private sector folks—developers, builders, real estate agents, the nonprofits and the people all of us serve. You got to get them into a room. You’ve got to get them all to go into the conversation. We put buyers and sellers together. We negotiate deals together. We put people together that maybe do not want to talk to one another, but they talk to people that get to the end result. And how you put those four pillars together is you show up to the meetings and you do what we all do every day very well, which is negotiate and put people together.

John Featherston: Why isn’t the real estate community playing a more pivotal role than every one of the communities, perhaps the most critical role as far as supporting homeownership?

Katey McGrath: I think everybody in this room is a connector. It’s what we love about what we do. And I think that our credibility and our expertise can help save money in these instances. The earlier we get involved in these conversations, the less mistakes the people with good intentions are going to make to make things more expensive. But also, the credibility that we have as a community member, right? So people are coming to us to buy houses, people are coming to us for education, people are coming to us to build generational wealth for their family. And then we can go to city council, we can go to the state reps, we can go to the zoning meetings and add all of that to just sort of be both sides of the issue.

Housing is bipartisan, so if you can get people together, you have that credibility, everything just becomes more productive and less expensive.

John Featherston: Getting people together over the issue of housing is a common denominator all of us can agree on. When Ennis said, local teachers, first responders, police, essential services, can’t afford to live in the community they’re serving, that is not a unique problem. That’s a problem that permeates around the nation. So the solutions have to be done locally.

Ennis Antoine: That’s correct. So I remind everyone, it doesn’t matter what company you work for, there is a percentage in every company where you deal with first-time homebuyers. It doesn’t have to be your main part of your business, but it should be a part of your business because to whom much is given, much is required. And we must always lead by example because the goal here is homeownership. What can we do? It’s all an education process. In every office, there are agents who are very passionate about their community. Support them, enhance them, show up when they need some help.

They will find a surgeon to have vibrant communities. We need a mixture of people in the community who are willing to work together, live together because we call it a village. And as real estate professionals, we are the fiber of the community. We are the voice of real estate and private property rights do matter.

Ken Baris: Something interesting we did some years ago, we listed 300 brand new two-family homes in Newark, New Jersey. And they were beautiful. The challenge was getting people in. So we had the builder prearrange financing, and we had them buy down the rate. And so it was a much lower interest rate, which lowered the payments. And then we also had the builder give a brand-new computer to every purchaser for one of the units because many of them were going to have kids and they would not have money left over for a new computer. And we thought that was very important. And then when certain buyers were tight, we then created a program where they were giving 5% toward closing costs, and if you get people to pay money toward closing costs, you also make it more affordable.

Keith Smith: “Connected” was the right word. I’ve been in this space for 15 years, and I would hear from elected officials, “Well, I don’t understand what the developer is saying.” The developer’s saying, “I don’t understand what the elected official is saying.” And then the nonprofits don’t understand anybody, and the poor folks that we’re trying to serve are left out there in the dark. Being a connector, that is the perfect word. That’s what we do. We connect and are able to put those four pillars together and put a developer and an elected official who really wants to do the right thing, together. The seller doesn’t trust the buyer; the buyer doesn’t trust the seller. We connect and we put people together and just get that conversation rolling. That’s how you do 40 new construction units in the second-highest cost-of-living area in the state of Virginia and everybody walks away smiling. I didn’t do anything really genius. I just put people in the room and said, start talking.

John Featherston: If you could do one thing to help move this affordability issue forward, what would you do?

Ennis Antoine: The one thing I would do is educate my colleagues and challenge them to get involved. It’s easy to write a check, but it’s amazing what happens when you show up. As a leader, when we show up, our people will follow. So as leaders, show up.

Ken Baris: I think just doing a better job of educating consumers because there’s a lot of buyers that don’t know their buyers. Not everyone will be able to afford. They’ve never always been able to, but there’s many more out there.

Katey McGrath: I’m going to go a little more philosophical. Just remember how important homeownership is to every metric in the community. It reduces crime. It increases education rates. It can lower the temperature in a zip code. It extends life expectancy, but most importantly, it builds generational wealth and creates health outcomes in that community that lift everybody up. And if you internalize that, you’re going to be happier when you go to work. You’re going to have more patience to deal with the hard clients that you’re not going to make a lot of money on and just feel great at the end of the day.

Keith Smith: Invest the one asset you can’t make more of, which is your time. Show up and over time, you’ll make a difference. And of those affordable housing units, every single key I handed to somebody, they were in tears. They never thought they were going to get into the homeownership game. It’s not about the check. It’s about the tears, and it makes a difference.

Beth McGuire is RISMedia’s vice president of online editorial.

CrowdStrike Launches AI Agent 'Kill Switch,' Plans to Acquire Fiddler AI

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

CrowdStrike Launches AI Agent 'Kill Switch,' Plans to Acquire Fiddler AI

CrowdStrike has introduced an AI agent kill switch designed to stop unauthorized activity inside corporate networks, alongside plans to acquire AI observability startup Fiddler as businesses give increasingly autonomous software access to their systems.

The cybersecurity company announced the launch Tuesday at its Fal.Con Europe 2026 conference in Barcelona, expanding its Falcon Guardian security platform with controls that let administrators intervene when AI agents stray beyond their assigned tasks.

The move tackles a problem companies face as they deploy AI systems capable of executing code, accessing sensitive information, and interacting with business applications. Unlike conventional chatbots, these agents can perform sequences of actions with limited human oversight, potentially causing damage even when nobody has deliberately attacked them.

In practice, an agent authorized to organize corporate files might misinterpret its instructions, follow malicious directions embedded in a document, or execute a command its operator never intended. The same access that makes autonomous agents useful can also make their mistakes expensive.

The new AI Kill Switch gives security administrators a way to interrupt an agent's activity without waiting for it to complete its task. According to CrowdStrike's technical explanation, the currently available mechanism works through AI gateways, which can block an agent's access to the models powering its decisions.

The initial release does not yet provide every type of containment CrowdStrike has described. The company plans to expand the feature to terminate agent processes directly on endpoints and coordinate restrictions across cloud environments and other enterprise systems.

An authorized administrator triggers the kill switch, and the action is recorded in an audit trail. CrowdStrike also describes controls to prevent terminated agents from restarting and restrict their network access, with broader enforcement capabilities still under development.

The company is adding protections at other points where agents operate. Falcon Guardian can inspect agent prompts and apply policies to tools including Claude Code, OpenAI Codex, GitHub Copilot, Microsoft 365 Copilot, Cursor, and OpenCode.

The risk is already well documented. As Decrypt previously reported, Microsoft researchers identified a vulnerability in Anthropic's Claude Code GitHub Action that could have allowed attackers to manipulate the coding agent into exposing sensitive credentials through malicious repository content. Anthropic subsequently patched the flaw but Crowdstrike’s solution would be more extensive.

CrowdStrike also signed an agreement to acquire Fiddler AI, a company specializing in monitoring AI models and explaining their behavior.

The deal values Fiddler at roughly $400 million, according to The Wall Street Journal. CrowdStrike has not publicly disclosed the acquisition price, although it said the transaction would be paid primarily in cash, with some stock subject to vesting conditions.

Fiddler's technology addresses a related problem that a kill switch alone cannot solve: understanding what an AI agent was doing before something went wrong, and whether its actions were appropriate in the first place.

Its software evaluates agents before deployment, traces their decisions and tool calls, and monitors changes in behavior after they begin operating. These records can help security teams investigate unexpected actions or provide evidence to auditors.

CrowdStrike expects to complete the Fiddler acquisition during the fourth quarter of fiscal 2027, which ends January 31, 2027, subject to customary closing conditions. Its expanded endpoint kill-switch capabilities have not yet received a specific release date.

Ubisoft reportedly among bidders for Dishonored and Marvel’s Blade dev Arkane from Microsoft

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Arkane may survive, but with a French twist.

Assassin’s Creed publisher Ubisoft is interested in acquiring the studio behind Dishonored and the in-progress Marvel’s Blade game, according to a new report.

Bloomberg’s Jason Schreier says Ubisoft is among “multiple” business entities that have made offers to Microsoft, along with “at least two other gaming companies,” according to anonymous sources familiar with negotiations.

The French connection is there for sure, with the Lyon, France-based Arkane garnering interest from the famously French publisher Ubisoft, so it may be a match made in baguette heaven. Sacre bleu!

A recent report stated that Arkane had just a few weeks to find a potential buyer or risk full closure, meaning hundreds of jobs would be at risk along with the future of the Blade game that’s been in the works for at least a few years. Earlier this year, Xbox announced it was looking to divest Arkane as part of CEO Asha Sharma’s “reset” of the company in efforts to make it profitable. A wave of thousands of layoffs has followed at numerous sections of Microsoft, including its gaming divisions and numerous studios within.

I’m sure many will have thoughts about Ubisoft taking on Arkane, with the studio going from one big-budget publisher to another, but if it means that jobs are saved and games continue to get made by people, then for now it could be considered a win if a deal gets made.

I am also quite interested to find out who the other companies looking to bid on Arkane may be, but Bloomberg’s sources could not garner more specific info.

Arkane has been responsible for several well-received games such as the Dishonored and Prey series, and Deathloop, but its most recent release was widely considered a flop. Redfall launched in 2023 and did not perform well in sales or with critics or players alike, so the heat is on for Blade to be a big one after it hasn’t been seen since its debut trailer nearly three years ago.

Google Messages redesigning pinned chats and moving search

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages is testing a redesign of pinned chats and search on the homepage.

Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

Otherwise, you’ll see a circular magnifying glass icon and “Start chat” in the pill. The animation that accompanies this change looks nice and feels alive.

This is a nice change for one-handed usage. It’s unclear if Google is removing the Gemini FAB with this update.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to testers.

Google’s Search Volume Is About 5x ChatGPT’s, OpenAI Says via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: State Of Agentic AEO For 2027: What’s Scaling & What’s Stalling

ChatGPT's search-like queries equal about a fifth of Google's volume, its maker says, counting each question that sends it to the web. Traffic data differs.

ChatGPT receives roughly one-fifth as many search-like queries as does Google, according to ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry.

The point he made about the volume of search-like queries was shared with Alex Kantrowitz in an open discussion during a Monday evening dinner in New York hosted by OpenAI. The transcript was later posted by Big Technology on Tuesday.

OpenAI counts a search each time a question sends ChatGPT to the web, Fry said. The visits ChatGPT sends to websites are a separate measure, and outside data on that traffic puts ChatGPT far behind Google.

Alex Kantrowitz of Big Technology asked Fry whether a conversation that includes a search counts as one, or whether each question requiring a web search counts separately. Fry replied:

“Every time you ask a question and go search the web, that’s a search.”

He said people first came to ChatGPT mostly for writing and coding help, which they wouldn’t have searched for. Over the past year, he said, they’ve brought questions about people, places and things that need up-to-date information, plus finance and health questions, where ChatGPT does a web search. He described the category as anything users would have taken to a traditional search engine.

Fry, in the published interview, did not reveal which Google figure was referenced; for how long the comparison is based on; nor does he state which regions are covered. He has also been asked if one-fifth of Google’s size makes ChatGPT multiple times bigger than Bing. The report by Big Technology indicates that the number of weekly active users of ChatGPT grew by 20% (from 1 billion to 1.2 billion) over the last two months.

In February, Ahrefs had estimated the search volume of ChatGPT to be around 12 percent that of Google’s search volume using a definition of search that was different from that used by OpenAI. It began with 2.5 billion daily ChatGPT prompts in July 2025 (from a study by Harvard University and OpenAI) and determined that approximately 65 percent were the type of query users would have previously looked up on Google.

Using this data, it compared those numbers with the over 5 trillion searches per year Google announced in March 2025. The author said that 65% was higher than the share the researchers had classified as search. That method sorts prompts by the kind of request, while Fry’s figure counts web searches.

Ahrefs also tracks where traffic to websites comes from across sites that use its free Web Analytics tool. In October, across 118,671 sites, Google accounted for 24.74% of tracked traffic and ChatGPT for 0.36%, according to the tracker’s Nov. 3 update.

Alphabet’s third-quarter release, which I covered on Oct. 28, gives Google’s search query growth as a rate rather than a count. In the release, CEO Sundar Pichai said AI investments are driving overall search query growth and that Google’s “year-over-year query growth rate is the highest it’s been in the last five years.”

OpenAI’s data comparing search-like queries by users of ChatGPT versus those using Google indicates the approximate 1/5th ratio. Fry’s counting rule includes only the search requests made by ChatGPT users in order to send a question to the Web. Ahrefs’ referral tracker tracks the number of visitors that are directed to websites. In this data set, Google has an overwhelming amount of referrals compared to ChatGPT.

Neither Fry’s number nor Google’s year-over-year growth rate illustrates if ChatGPT searches are replacing searches on Google. If ChatGPT’s size is included in a channel plan, then when reporting search it should be accompanied by the definitions of query volume and referral traffic, which will provide two different metrics.

Ahrefs’ tracker reports referral shares month by month, giving you a regular outside perspective on the traffic ChatGPT sends. OpenAI’s roughly one-fifth ratio is difficult to compare with other estimates until the company clarifies which Google figure it used.

Follow on YouTube. Matt G. Southern is the Senior News Writer at Search Engine Journal, where he’s covered Google, SEO, ...

Getting to Know New York Through Its Sandwiches

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

“By the end of our time together, I will be asking you again: ‘So, what is a sandwich?’”

An expert-led tour that starts by questioning its own object of inquiry is unconventional. But then, so is our guide. Ben Gollan, known to his Instagram followers as A Man and His Sandwich, has spent the last nine years reviewing sandwiches across New York City. Gollan moved here from Australia in 2016, after nearly a decade as a government lawyer. For about as long, he has run an Airbnb Experience that turns a food crawl into a two-hour pilgrimage to the city’s best sandwich slingers.

It was a pastrami sandwich at Harry & Ida’s, a former deli in the East Village, that turned Gollan into a professional sandwich hound. “It was a fourth-generation Jewish deli that broke every rule of pastrami sandwiches: it was thick-cut and fabulous,” he says as we dash across Bowery. We’re scuttling over from a coffee shop in Cooper Square that served as our meeting point and into the East Village—a neighborhood of contrasts that still manages to connect New York's past and present. There are 12 of us in total, mostly locals, a couple of newer transplants, and just one traveler. “It’s usually mostly visitors, so this is different,” he says.

It’s a great introduction to the city. After all, New York runs on sandwiches, and there’s no shortage of great ones, whether it’s a classic Italian deli sub you’re after or a grab-and-go banh mi. They’re economical, versatile, delicious and, as is the point of Gollan’s tour, telegraph the city’s greatest personality trait—its diversity. So is there such a thing as a New York sandwich? “Not really,” he says, "but the chopped cheese, born in East Harlem, might come closest.”

The Bono at Via Della Scrofa, a small family-owned joint that wouldn't look out of place on the streets of Rome

“We start here because it’s closest to what we think of as a traditional sandwich,” says Gollan, before filling us in on the origins of what is the modern-day sandwich. It involves John Montagu, the 4th Earl of Sandwich, who, in the 18th century, gave his title to the now-ubiquitous food. Gollan loves to talk and think about sandwiches as much as he eats them. “Although I've learned never to trust history, especially when rendered by colonizers,” he muses. “Do we really think it took us till the 1700s to invent the sandwich?”

Named after a Roman street, Via Della Scrofa is the kind of bottega or alimentari you might find in Rome, with a tiny counter selling tiny espressos and focaccia, as well as imported groceries from Italy. “It's small family-owned joints like this that make New York what it is,” says Gollan. While the rest of the group gets to try the Porcodeddena panini (made with porchetta), I’m getting a Bono: grilled eggplants sweetened with sun-dried tomatoes, with arugula and buffalo mozzarella, in a warm roll that’s toasty on the outside and soft and warm on the inside. The bread is perfect—it’s a rosetta (or michetta), a traditional Roman bread roll. I wash it down with a Crodino, a popular non-alcoholic bitter aperitif from Italy.

Around us, the Lower East Side Arts & Culture Open House, an annual event, had taken over the street, adding personality to our time with its bands, pop-up stalls, and lively crowds. Gollan regales us with stories of owner Giovanni Bartocci, describing him as a real character with “a man bun, suspenders, tattoos, and naturally, a Harley," and I look around hoping he'll show up. I ask Gollan why he isn’t eating with us. “I never want to tire of these sandwiches, and if I keep eating them, I will.” The wind picks up and blows over the canvases at an art stall, but the band is undeterred, playing “New York, New York.”

A spicy Pakistani rendition of the chopped cheese at Nishaan

The writer and her chopped cheese sandwich, the chapli kebab replaced with Impossible Meat.

Order: A chopped cheese, but make it desi

An 11-minute walk up 1st Avenue and a few blocks later, we spot our next sandwich spot. A cobalt-blue sign reads Nishaan; bags of rice are piled high along its exterior; and a long line snakes out of what I can only assume is a tiny space inside. Nishaan began as a pop-up at the weekly open-air food festival Smorgasburg and went on to win season 18 of The Great Food Truck Race. While there are other dishes on the menu (elote chaat, mango lassi), it’s Nishaan’s twist on that great New York City bodega staple, the chopped cheese, that many are after: a spice-laden chapli kebab, chopped up on a griddle with peppers and onions, then enhanced with a zingy cilantro sauce and tamarind chutney. When it opened in August 2025, it was one of the most talked-about new restaurants in New York City, which has emerged as a hub for a new generation of Pakistani eateries. “New York belongs to everyone, and so does the sandwich,” proclaims Gollan.

In a city that runs on pastrami, vegetarians like me don’t faze Gollan; he orders me a version with Impossible Meat. We tuck ourselves out of the wind and into an alley and dig in. I can’t stop eating, making a mess as I go along. “Half the fun of a sandwich is the experience of eating it,” Gollan adds.

Regulars love the pho sandwiches at Sunny and Annie’s—sandwiches stuffed to the brim with pho ingredients, minus the broth.

Sunny and Annie’s is a 24/7, 365-day-a-year kind of neighborhood classic with 92 items on its sandwich menu.

“We’re taking a detour,” says Gollan, frantically looking around him. It’s the day of the Tompkins Square Halloween Dog Parade, and the crowds around us are building, as are the dogs in costume—cheese boards, UPS drivers, and, suitably for the occasion, hot dogs. We ditch the idea of cutting through the park and work our way around the revelers.

Our destination? Sunny and Annie’s, a 24/7, 365-days-a-year kind of neighborhood classic that has been open since 1997. There’s no front door here, because there’s always someone around. Inside, past the candy, chips, and dish detergent, we’re greeted by what must be the most extensive sandwich menu I’ve ever encountered, all written out on brightly colored sheets of paper stuck to the glass counter. There are 92 handheld options in total, some named after politicians and celebrities, like the Jackie Chan—bulgogi, cilantro, avocado, lettuce, jalapeños, and onions, served on a roll or hero. There’s also a chicken cutlet sandwich called the Australian. It’s not named after Gollan.

This is not a place to linger, so we keep it moving. Outside, the crowds have thickened, and Gollan instructs us to head to a neighborhood bar called ABC Beer Co., which often features at the end of his tours. As someone who has spent many a night in an East Village bar, I’m disappointed I’ve never been here before. It’s spacious, has a backyard, and is equally welcoming to couples on dates and families with little ones.

The writer's sandwich tour ended at ABC Beer Co, a hybrid beer bar and store with a particular focus on NYC breweries.

Gollan appears with our pho sandwiches, wrapped in spinach tortillas. They smell like pho, taste like pho; only the avocado throws me off.

Some of us get ciders and wine, and the conversation winds back to where we started: So, what really is a sandwich? “I want this to get contentious: bang the tables, let’s have a food fight," Gollan says, egging us on. Hot dogs? Quesadillas? What about a hamburger? A pizza folded in half? Does it have to have a carb? What about a lettuce wrap instead of bread? Gollan briefs us on a famous 2006 Massachusetts lawsuit—White City Shopping Center vs. Panera Bread—which legally answered an age-old culinary debate: Is a burrito a sandwich? The judge ruled that a burrito is, in fact, not a sandwich, clearing the way for the Mexican fast-casual joint to open in the vicinity of Panera Bread. “A sandwich court case is my Roman Empire," he says. “When this question goes to the Supreme Court, I will be there.”

The point of this tour—and the posed question—Gollan says, was never to walk away with an answer, but to open our minds and palates. So, what are Ben Gollan’s own rules for a sandwich, I have to ask. He lists the following:

And then he adds: “Take those rules and throw them in the bin.” And with that, class is dismissed.

The NYC Sandwich Tour can be booked directly on the A Man and His Sandwich website, as well as on Airbnb. Tours run every day of the week, with the exception of Tuesdays and Wednesdays. Ben Gollan, who proudly calls himself the Chief Sandwich Officer, often leads the experiences himself, but he also has co-hosts to help guide fellow sandwich enthusiasts on these two-hour outings. Along the way, guests sample three standout sandwiches—from the classic to the “slightly unhinged”—before finishing up at a bar to settle the debate over what really counts as a sandwich. While my tour focused on the East Village, Gollan also leads sandwich tours around Williamsburg. And word on the street is that he has his eye on opening a sandwich shop of his own one day.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Realtors Predict These Nine Cities Will Be Unaffordable by 2027

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Vibrant-Colored neon lights on a busy night at the bars and honky-tonks in the country music center, Broadway, the tourism center of Downtown Nashville, Tennessee

(TNS)–Home prices are climbing across the nation and some markets are hotter than others. According to a new report from Finance Buzz, nine U.S. cities are likely to become unaffordable by 2027, due to an influx of out-of-town buyers and tight inventory.

Here’s a look at the popular locales where housing costs are outpacing local incomes, as per Finance Buzz’s reporting:

Many areas in northern New Jersey are popular for people relocating out of New York City.

According to Daniel Smith, team lead at Smith Realty Team, homes in Montclair are expected to stay in high demand and continue commanding higher home prices.

“Retirees and pre-retirees are also targeting these towns because of their vibrant downtowns, access to health care, and cultural amenities, which makes competition even stronger,” he said.

Nashville attracted numerous new residents during the pandemic years, offering a lively city experience alongside lower taxes and a more affordable cost of living.

While home prices have come down slightly over the past year, the median sale price is currently $464,983 and real estate experts don’t expect a reduction any time soon.

Realtor Carla Gericke, with Porcupine Real Estate, said that “no income tax, easy access to Boston, and that elusive small-town feel with real community bones” is causing Nashua’s popularity to spike.

“Nashua’s median home price has soared to the $500,000 to $550,000 range, and demand keeps outpacing supply,” she said.

Gericke urged retirees looking to buy in the state to act quickly or venture outside the bigger cities to find better deals.

Arizona has long been a top destination for retirees hoping to escape the cold. Fred Loguidice, a real estate expert and founder of several home-buying companies in the area, said that home demand is outpacing supply, making it a market that is likely to stay competitive in the future.

“In markets like Phoenix, fewer homes are being built while demand persists, so prices consistently escalate,” he said.

Real estate expert Fred Loguidice also highlighted demand for Northeast hubs like the Buffalo metro area. While Zillow put the median home price at a moderate $261,300, home values in the area have been increasing.

“That momentum is a hard habit to break,” he said, noting its affordability and surging job opportunities make it a top choice for relocation for people in the region and beyond.

Athens is about 60 miles northeast of Atlanta and attracts many buyers and visitors alike with its lively college town atmosphere, excellent dining scene, and more.

While Georgia is often cited as a top spot to retire, thanks to the weather and lower cost of living, real estate trends indicate that home prices will continue to climb throughout the next year or so. Prices have gone up over the past year, with the median currently sitting at $353,333.

Florida boasts many affordable cities and tax incentives for retirees, but those considering Tampa may want to reassess.

Omer Reiner, realtor and president of Florida Cash Home Buyers, said home prices in the city are already high, and rising insurance costs are putting many popular areas out of reach.

“This is especially true for those nearing or at retirement, as higher costs of living when you are on any type of fixed income means you are spending more for less,” he said.

Iowa isn’t particularly known for its excessive home prices, but demand has been on the rise in Des Moines, according to real estate investor Jacob Naig, particularly for homes popular with retirees.

“Single-family homes, as well as small multi-family properties and single-level ranch-style homes that are particularly desirable to aging buyers, have seen a substantial rise in demand,” he said.

Many cities in the Pacific Northwest have seen rapidly rising home prices, and Ontario, which sits on the border of Oregon and Idaho, is no different. With stunning desert views, mountain ranges, and more, it’s a haven for outdoorsy types.

But recent real estate trends indicated it could be unaffordable in the coming years. Average home prices have risen steadily over the past year and currently sit at $307,769.

Grumpylocks and the Three Bears

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The Ice Age Trail’s camping options are a little more restrictive than you might expect. Even the western region has plenty of segments where you can’t simply hit your miles for the day and just find the nearest flat spot. Here’s where I spent my nights on the IAT.

While some trailheads on the IAT had “No Overnight Parking/Camping” signs, the four where I stayed did not. Just four days into my hike, I camped at a trailhead next to a quiet dirt road where traffic wasn’t the reason I kept waking up. Heat, humidity, and stagnant air were the problem.

There was still no hint of a breeze by morning, and I was sweating before I left camp. I was filtering water at the next trailhead when a pair of day-hikers arrived. They parked their car, hit the trail, and it was probably half an hour before I caught up with them. They smelled nice and clean: a scent I picked up at least a minute before I saw them again. That’s how still the air was. I’m sure I smelled fine.

That morning’s trail was lined with some sort of tall grass which was shedding tiny disk-like seeds that stuck to my sweaty skin. By the time I reached the road to Haugen, my arms and legs had the texture of 60 grit sandpaper.

The three miles of blacktop between me and my intended destination were hot: probably the most uncomfortable of the entire trail. The Shady Rest Campground was about an hour away, and I was looking forward to a cool shower and a cold drink. FarOut comments even mentioned that they have air-conditioned cabins. When I arrived, the office was closed. It was a Monday.

Fortunately, one of the owner’s friends was nearby. She called the owner, who rented me a cabin. She also gave me a ride into town and some quarters for the shower. Again, I’m sure I smelled fine.

At Lona’s Corner Cafe, I sat as far as possible from the other customers. I discovered a tick on my arm during lunch. As casually and covertly as possible, I removed the tick using the array of utensils in front of me, making sure it would never bite anyone else.

After a quick stop at the grocery store for five days’ worth of food, I walked back to the campground to take a shower. Once I’d completed my chores, I literally chilled in my cabin for the rest of the afternoon.

I visited the store again on my way out of town the next morning. After breakfast on the bench across the street, I headed south on the Wild Rivers State Trail, then east on the Tuscobia State Trail. Both trails are wide (former railroads) but I was still a little surprised to be overtaken by a horse and buggy. At the end of the day, with no better options, I stopped at another trailhead.

The mosquitoes were hungry the following morning. I was carrying a small bottle of DEET for emergency use, but I wasn’t sure the situation qualified. I’d encountered worse. Still, after eight itchy miles, I wanted a break. I left the trail at the north end of Stout Road and enjoyed an hour of bug-free bliss. Even the hound that bounded beyond its house’s boundary to hound me wasn’t really a problem, despite its loud, raspy bark. It was never going to bite. It was, as they say, “All mouth and no trousers.”

I know some people hike every inch of official trail, but I need flexibility during a thru-hike. This wouldn’t be the last time I deviated from the trail, and in the end, my way was no more arbitrary than the IAT itself. The Blue Hills segments I hiked that day have since been re-routed, reducing the number of official trail miles.

This was to be my first of many days that were more road than trail. No surprise, the air was still and the mosquitoes voracious. I used some of my precious DEET to shield me while I broke camp and hiked 2.6 miles. For most of that distance, the trail crossed private land, and I could smell the cows long before I saw them. After an hour, I started a road-walk that would last all day.

At the gas station in Weyerhaeuser, I topped-up on fuel and coolant. On the other side of town, I spotted an animal that I initially thought was a large dog. I’d never seen such a lean, long-legged bear. She saw me and hurried across the road with two cubs close behind.

Eventually, the scenery started to change. Isolated farms were replaced with frequent lakeside houses. Having covered 21 miles by 1 PM, I stopped for a break at Round Lake County Park. I was way ahead of schedule, just three miles from the DCA were I intended to camp.

The IAT passes plenty of state, county, and city parks, each of which is a gift when you’re a thru-hiker. They typically have water, picnic tables, a pavilion, electrical outlets, trash cans, and pit (sometimes flush) toilets. Round Lake was my first county park on the IAT. I spent three hours at a picnic table in the pavilion, and used all the amenities. I also spoke to Paul and Cindy, the first of many hikers I’d meet who were hiking the entire IAT, one piece at a time. They offered to leave a beer for me at a local bar, and I eagerly accepted. This was a trail-magic first.

I hung out until four, went for a swim, rinsed my clothes, and wrung them out. They were almost dry by the time I arrived at The King’s Inn, two miles later. Paul and Cindy had indeed bought me a beer. I had three more with dinner, then walked one more mile to my campsite. Not a typical day, but an enjoyable one.

The morning was cool and overcast, and a light rain started at midday. A runner training for a 50k overtook me, slowing for a few minutes to chat before continuing his out-and-back. For the next 90 minutes, the rain just kept getting heavier. Eventually, on a wide section of trail, I put up my tent in “shelter” mode: footprint and rainfly only. I’d applied seam-sealant before my hike, but I discovered that the fly still leaked. The runner, Jacob, returned 45 minutes later. There was enough room in my makeshift shelter for two, even if it was a little cramped. We went our separate ways an hour after that, once the rain began to ease. It was still sprinkling when I reached my DCA an hour later, but a mylar heat-sheet beneath the leaky fly successfully redirected the drips.

Before leaving Chippewa County for Taylor County, I had a decision to make. If I spent two or three days on the IAT, I’d hike more than 40 miles of road and only half a mile of trail.

If I made my own way, I’d hike 25 miles of busy state highway and zero miles of trail. I chose option one.

I arrived in Cornell just after 9 AM, and the mayor pulled over to chat as I was walking down Main Street. After breakfast at Sam’s Place, I bought a five day resupply and hit the road at 11 o’clock. I stopped at Hidden Shady Nook campground mid-afternoon. The bartender introduced me to Spotted Cow beer, and I enjoyed a few air-conditioned hours overlooking Pike Lake.

The next day, Sunday, was even shorter. I left at 7 AM, and reached the south end of Otter Lake in less than four hours. The boat ramp was busy and the parking lot was half full. I ate some snacks while filtering water, walked a quarter mile back to the trailhead, and 0.6 trail miles to the DCA. The day was warm, with no rain in the forecast, so I didn’t bother with the rain fly. I put up my tent in the shade of an impressive tree, and settled down to read.

An hour later, although I didn’t hear anyone approach, I heard a voice say flatly, “Oh.” I raised my head just in time to see a guy turn around and leave. He was dressed more like a golfer than a hiker, and he wasn’t carrying anything. I guessed he was scouting the campsite before bringing gear and/or people.

At 4:30 PM, I had another visitor. This one wasn’t using the trail, and made no effort to hide its approach through dead leaves and dry brush. I raised my head again, and turned awkwardly to my right. An adult black bear. Realization took a second, but during that second, she continued to move deliberately, directly towards me.

“THAT’S CLOSE ENOUGH!” I was nearly at full volume.

She stopped immediately, calmly, almost casually. She avoided looking in my direction, but stayed where she was, about 20 yards away. Then, almost in unison, two furry little faces popped up out of the brush next to her. It was the cubs’ turn to take a moment. While they gathered their thoughts, I sat bolt upright. The nearest cub sprinted in my general direction.

Fortunately, the cub hadn’t been overcome by curiosity. It wasn’t helpless to resist the aroma of dried apricots coming from my bear canister. It had simply decided that, without checking for any alternatives, there was only one suitable tree in the vicinity. That tree was about 10 yards from my tent, a yard or two off the direct line of sight between me and its mother.

The sound of claws on tree-bark was surprisingly loud. Also, that little bear could climb surprisingly quickly. The second cub was close behind, just as quick, and climbed just as loudly. They went high enough that I couldn’t see or hear them.

Momma bear didn’t react when the cubs’ instincts/training kicked in. I didn’t see any change in her body language. She wasn’t aggressive, agitated, or even concerned. She just stood there, on all fours, shuffling a little from left to right, looking anywhere but at me.

They all needed some space, I decided. I narrated what I was doing as I unzipped my tent, put on my flipflops, and zipped up the door. I continued talking as I unscrewed the lid of my bear can, stuffed my toiletries into it, and screwed the lid back down. Click. Click. There wasn’t enough space in the canister for my evening meal, so I took it with me.

Armed with two blocks of ramen, I retreated down a short spur trail marked by little yellow flags. After about 20 yards, I shut the f*ck up, turned around, and waited. I was close enough to intervene if the bear went for my stuff, but far enough that the cubs would come down from the tree. That was the plan, anyway.

Momma was no longer interested in the canister, or any of my gear. It took her about 30 minutes to shuffle slowly, hesitantly to the base of the tree. She stood on her hind legs, front paws against the trunk, looking up, trying to coax the cubs down. I couldn’t see or hear any movement from above. All I could hear were Momma’s gentle calls, repeated every minute or two. Each call sounded like a sigh mixed with a groan, followed by three short exhalations through pursed lips. It was interesting, but ineffective. The cubs weren’t coming down.

By now, I’d accumulated 45 minutes’ worth of mosquito bites, and I’d run out of patience. I turned my narration back on and flipflopped back to my tent.

Breaking camp took about 15 minutes – a new personal record. I bundled everything haphazardly into my pack while giving a grumpy commentary. Mom, meanwhile, was still giving words of encouragement to the cubs.

I didn’t see the bears again, despite spending the night at the boat-ramp parking lot, only a quarter mile from the DCA as the crow flies.

This wasn’t a habituated bear, it was a food-conditioned bear. I don’t think she just happened to pick up the scent of my bear canister. With only one exception, all black bears I’ve previously encountered have been quick to show me their big jiggly ass when they ran away. This bear was brazen enough to come into camp in broad daylight, so even if she’d run away, she was probably coming back at night. The cubs did a fine job of distracting her from her mission.

I wonder how many times golfer-guy had hiked to the DCA only to find it occupied? Enough to justify a 1.2 mile round trip, apparently. Surely there can’t be that many IAT hikers using the DCA. And even if IAT hikers were always camping there, they probably know how to store their food properly. It seems likely that the DCA is frequented by non-outdoorsy types who don’t know that “A fed bear is a dead bear.”

At the very least, a fed bear is a f*cking nuisance. Its cubs might grow up to be just as much of a problem. I’m probably preaching to the choir when I tell you to store your pic-a-nic baskets properly.

I added a FarOut comment about the bear, and submitted a trail alert on the IATA website. I used a little less detail for those though.

Wisconsin road users (that snake’s fake, btw).

Earlier this year, JetBlue raised its checked bag fees and kicked off a wave of fee hikes across the industry. United followed. Then Delta, American and Southwest.

Seven months later, JetBlue is leading the fee-hiking charge again.

The carrier on Tuesday raised its bag fees for the second time in 2026.

Going forward, customers will pay at least $50 per bag. That’s a 28% jump from the lowest fee the airline previously charged its customers.

Passengers who don’t pre-pay for their bag will pay $60 for their first piece of checked luggage.

And because JetBlue uses a dynamic baggage-pricing system, flyers will pay even more for their checked baggage on peak travel days.

In a statement about the airline's second bag-fee hike this year, the carrier pointed to rising fuel and operating costs.

“This helps us keep our base fares competitive while giving customers the choice to pay for a checked bag only if they need one,” a spokesperson said.

Here’s how JetBlue’s bag fees will shake out, going forward.

These fees will apply to flights booked starting Nov. 3.

So if you had already purchased a JetBlue flight prior to Nov. 3 — including trips for the holidays — the previous fees would apply to your trip.

As noted above, JetBlue also raised the fees customers will pay for their second checked bag, too.

Here are the days where JetBlue's "peak" baggage fees will apply, through October 2027.

JetBlue does continue to offer free baggage perks as a benefit of certain cobranded credit cards. Plus, its Mosaic elite status members each get at least one free bag.

Customers flying in JetBlue’s premium Mint cabin are still entitled to their first bag free. The same goes for flyers who book the carrier’s new domestic first-class (BlueFirst) seats, which are set to debut on some flights next month.

As JetBlue raises its baggage fees, the biggest question for consumers is how the rest of the airline industry respond.

If history is any indication, we’re about to see another wave of fee hikes across the industry.

After JetBlue raised its bag fees in late March, it took only weeks for nearly every major U.S. carrier to follow with their own fee hikes.

This would be the latest case of sticker shock for travelers, who are simultaneously battling surging fares.

Even budget airline Frontier last week reported customers had paid 71% more in fares over the prior quarter.

All of this comes as airlines have had to contend with sky-high jet fuel costs in 2026 — which they’ve aggressively passed onto customers.

But while jet fuel costs may eventually fall, it’s unlikely flyers will see any future relief when it comes to baggage fees — typically, once those go up, they don’t come back down.

While JetBlue has raised its baggage fees, it is worth noting that passengers do have freedom to bring a full-size carry-on bag on JetBlue flights, regardless of the fare type. The airline no longer bars its Blue Basic (basic economy) passengers from using the overhead bins.

Of course, the belongings packed in any carry-on bag must meet the Transportation Security Administration's 3-1-1 liquids policy.

Hiding inside Palantir’s skyrocketing quarter is a foreboding signal

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

The data analysis company Palantir delivered such a huge third-quarter earnings report on Monday that its press release even bragged that the numbers were “crushing consensus expectations.”

Yet retail investors were lukewarm on the company on Tuesday, with the stock trading slightly down. That’s because Palantir’s enterprise sales, its big growth area, showed signs of slowing.

First, the good news for the company’s investors. Revenue grew 96% from a year earlier, and 20% from the previous quarter, to $2.31 billion, beating analysts’ expectations of $2.18 billion. It was the first time Palantir has topped $2 billion in a quarter. Palantir also reported adjusted earnings per share (profit per share, excluding certain one-time items) of 44 cents, compared with analysts’ expectations of 41 cents. The company even raised its revenue guidance, or forecast, for the full year. Normally, that alone would send a stock soaring.

The clincher was that Palantir’s U.S. commercial revenue surpassed U.S. government revenue for the first time ever. The company reported $984 million in commercial contracts (up 148% from the year-ago quarter), versus $953 million in U.S. government revenue.

The reason for all this love from enterprises? They are signing up for the company’s AI software that can run frontier models and custom-train open-weight models securely, without sharing any of their data and business processes with outside AI labs. Plus, Palantir has long offered what is today known as “forward-deployed” software engineering, in which its own engineers work directly with customers to customize the software.

However, Palantir also revealed a less-than-rosy signal: Even as enterprises are moving faster than ever, they are often signing short-term trials.

“What Ryan and I are seeing on the front line and commercial is this much quicker interaction, much quicker to deployment, shorter duration of the contract but then an ability to expand that contract dramatically,” CEO Alex Karp (pictured above) told Wall Street analysts on the quarterly earnings call.

Palantir is experiencing one of the unique phenomena of AI in the enterprise right now. Corporations are willing to quickly try AI software, skipping the usual bureaucracy, but they are also more willing to cancel contracts.

Karp used one off-color metaphor after another to describe how unconcerned he was about holding onto his enterprise business.

“We’ve gone from being the most attractive person in the physics department to being the captain of the football team, kind of thing,” he said, describing the influx of prospective corporate customers flowing in. “Conversely, you know, once you go Palantir, you don’t go back, and that’s just how it is, and you see it in our numbers.”

The numbers don’t yet confirm Palantir’s long-term success. While the company closed a hefty $1.46 billion of total contract value from U.S. enterprises in the quarter, that’s actually a decline from the record $2.13 billion in the previous quarter. That suggests slowing sales, at least in the most recent quarter.

The next earnings will show whether Palantir has solidified its role as a rising U.S. enterprise AI provider. Historically, many enterprises sign their biggest tech purchases in the final quarter of the year. Karp and his team certainly sound confident. They increased guidance for U.S. commercial revenue to be “in excess of $3.559 billion, representing a growth rate of at least 143%.”
