Peak Design’s new wireless charging stand pulls double duty, literally

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The three new charging accessories from Peak Design hit the high notes, like (dual) Qi2 wireless charging and wired fast charging without bulky bricks.

Last year at CES, Peak Design gave us a sneak preview of its upcoming accessory lineup. Obviously, the company is known for its premium travel bags and accessories, and they make some awesome gear. But they have mobile accessories, too, and the latest release looks pretty cool.

All three new products are charging accessories that work with Android. More specifically, they support the right protocols, like Qi2 25W wireless charging and 65W fast wired charging.

In fact, the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station supports dual, simultaneous Qi2 charging. In essence, it’s an adjustable charging stand that either lays flat or props up for a more traditional experience. Premium build materials like aluminum are used for the base, top cradle, and stem. There’s a recessed USB-C port for the adapter you’ll need to source yourself, and a secondary USB-C output port for charging a third device.

To get full power from the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station, you’ll need at minimum a 75W charging adapter and cable. The box includes a 100W cable, but charging two devices at 25W wirelessly takes a lot of power. Still, it’s pretty cool that it packs two Qi2 pads, not just one paired with a slower coil.

If that’s not your use case, a 65W charger is enough to power one pad at the full 25W. The absolute minimum required power to charge anything is 45W, Peak Design says on its website.

That might be why Peak Design also launched a slim 65W travel charger. It’s a fantastic little charger that slips into a tech pouch or backpack without much trouble. I carry a slim 65W charger wherever I go, and this easily fits the bill for portability.

It has two USB-C ports. Only one is rated for 65W, and if both are in use, you’re either getting 30W/30W or 45W/20W output. Still, I don’t demand much from a travel charger, and 65W is plenty fast enough to charge a MacBook Air or Pixel 11 within the hour.

The other option is a slim battery. Peak Design also launched a Qi2 Kickstand Travel Battery, capable of 25W wireless fast charging as well. It’s rated for 5,000mAh. Because of the kickstand that wraps around and acts as a frame for the charger, it isn’t as slim as some of the batteries we’re seeing recently. Still, I wouldn’t call it “thick” by any means.

It’s solidly built and has a really nice faux leather finish on both sides, accented by status LEDs on the bottom edge. It also sits really nicely on the Pixel 11 I used it on, which is a point of failure for some. This one feels really nice to use.

Peak Design’s new charging accessories don’t come cheap. The new travel 2-in-1 charging stand is available for $99.95, but that’s really not that bad if you value dual fast wireless charging or have a secondary device or partner to use it with. The battery comes at the same price of $99.95, and the slim 65W charger is available for $64.95.

The Founding Father of Neuroscience on Solitude, the Search for Truth, and the Ideal Environment for Breakthroughs

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

“The real wealth of the Nation lies in the resources of the earth… Their administration is not properly, and cannot be, a matter of politics,” trailblazing scientist and writer Rachel Carson presciently admonished in 1953 as she made the case for protecting science, nature, and thus humanity itself from greedy and destructive political agendas. “The scientific way of thinking,” Carl Sagan wrote in his final published masterwork, “is … an essential tool for a democracy in an age of change.”

Half a century before Carson and a century before Sagan, neuroscience founding father Santiago Ramón y Cajal (May 1, 1852–October 17, 1934) considered the crucial role of science in a nation’s welfare and greatness in his book Advice for a Young Investigator (public library) — the science counterpart to Rilke’s Letters to a Young Poet and Anna Deavere Smith’s Letters to a Young Artist, and the source of Cajal’s insightful taxonomy of the six “diseases of the will” that keep the talented from achieving greatness.

Today’s statesmen undoubtedly have limitations, one of which is not realizing (or at least not advocating) that the greatness and might of nations are products of science, and that justice, order, and good laws are important but secondary factors in prosperity.

But science, of course, only thrives when scientists thrive. For science to steer a society toward greatness, Cajal cautions, that society must nurture an optimal intellectual and moral environment for its inhabitants. He writes:

Like all mental activities, the accomplishments of the scientist are heavily influenced by the physical and moral environments around him. It has been said, with good reason, that the man of learning is like a delicate plant that only thrives in a special medium — soil deposited by the culture of centuries and tilled by society’s care and esteem. In favorable surroundings, even the backward type has a feeling of accomplishment, whereas in a hostile or indifferent environment even the sharpest mind is discouraged… Only the stern and heroic have the strength to overcome adverse environmental conditions and wait in obscure resignation for the approval of posterity. But society must not count on heroes because there may be no opportunity for them to appear. Instead, we must rely on people with average skills and ordinary talents who are inspired with a noble patriotism and clear ambition.

Cajal considers what conditions create such an opportunity for the blossoming of noble brilliance. With the spirited conviction of one whose own life is a testament to this truth, he points to solitude — that supreme fertilizer of creative work — as chief among them:

Our major commitment … is to discover ourselves before discovering scientific truth, to mold ourselves before molding nature. To fashion a strong brain, an original mind that is ours alone — this is the preliminary work that is absolutely essential.

Echoing the central message of Hemingway’s Nobel Prize acceptance speech, he adds:

Oh comforting solitude, how favorable thou art to original thought! How satisfying and rewarding are the long winter evenings spent in the private laboratory, at the very time when educational centers are closed to their workers! Such evenings free us from poorly thought out improvisations, strengthen our patience, and refine our powers of observation. What care we zealously lavish on our own instruments — each one representing a vanity disowned or a bad habit unindulged! Because we love them we appreciate their fine points, we are aware of their defects, and we avoid the traps they occasionally set for us. In short, we understand their friendly soul, which always responds humbly and quickly to our needs.

From the point of view of actual success, it is not the instruments that are costly and require the most time, work, and patience — as we have already pointed out, it is the development and maturing of talent.

Complement this particular portion of the wholly illuminating Advice for a Young Investigator with Bob Dylan on the ideal environment for creative work and astrophysicist Janna Levin on what motivates scientists, then revisit Cajal’s revolutionary drawings of the brain.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

The One 737 Variant That Saved Boeing's Narrowbody Line

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Today, the aircraft is known for being the second-best-selling commercial jetliner of all time, behind only the competing Airbus A320. In the late 1970s, however, the Boeing 737 was not the dominant narrowbody that everyone knows it to be today. That was the Boeing 727, a larger, three-engined aircraft used for medium-length flights around the world, similar in size to modern 737 variants. Back then, the 737 was Boeing's small narrowbody, essentially used like a large regional jet, while the 727 dominated the industry.

In the late 1970s, however, the 727's three-engined layout was increasingly becoming obsolete, as the plane burned too much fuel for its size and range. Boeing would respond by developing an all-new, twin-engined layout that essentially did everything the 727 did but better. At the same time, there was demand for an improved 737 after the success of the 737-200. As such, Boeing began studies on a new 737 variant in 1979, and in 1984, the company released the massively successful 737-300.

By 1979, the 737 line was essentially the 737-200 Advanced. Slightly stretched from the original 737-100, the 737-200 became a popular choice worldwide for regional routes, competing against the McDonnell Douglas DC-9. The Advanced became the production standard in 1971, incorporating aerodynamic and technical improvements to improve payload and range. It was a massive success, but the 737 was also aging by the late 1970s, creating demand for an improved version.

The most noticeable difference with the 737-300 was its extended fuselage, increasing passenger capacity and lowering unit costs. The aircraft's wing was tweaked for better aerodynamics. Boeing swapped out the 1960s-era Pratt & Whitney JT8Ds for the new CFM56-3 high-bypass turbofan, and the plane received a new vertical stabilizer design, while the cabin and flight deck were modernized. As a whole, the 737-300 was a significantly more capable, more efficient, and more advanced version of the already popular 737.

The 737-300 proved to be a very successful aircraft, with Boeing receiving hundreds of orders for the type after its entry into service in 1984. At this point, the aircraft was competing mainly against the McDonnell Douglas MD-80, but it was more fuel-efficient while carrying slightly fewer passengers, and it was used by many airlines to replace older 727s as well. In the 1980s, the market also lined up well for the 737-300 to become a core part of Boeing's narrowbody offering alongside the 757.

By the late 1970s, both of Boeing's two narrowbody products were popular, but aging. The 737 was revitalized as the 737-300, while the airframer decided on a clean-sheet twinjet to succeed the 727. The 757 carried more passengers and payload than the 727, while having superior takeoff performance, a longer range, and better fuel burn. It was an appealing aircraft for airlines wanting an aircraft larger than the 737, and it also proved effective in replacing 707s and DC-8s on transcontinental flights in the US, given its range.

However, the 757 wasn't as desirable as Boeing may have hoped when it debuted in 1983. The US market began shifting towards smaller aircraft, and the 757 seated more passengers than the 727. Fuel prices also dropped, and the 757 was an expensive aircraft to buy.

Many airlines chose to simply keep their depreciated 727s longer, while others bought the cheaper MD-80, which also entered service earlier than the 757. The 737-300 became a crucial aircraft, as it occupied this space of being more fuel-efficient than prior jetliners, while also being smaller and cheaper to buy than the 757. It became a popular replacement for the 727 as a result.

Orders for the 757 picked up in the late 1980s, and the aircraft went on to be a successful program. However, it was during this time with the 737-300 that the aircraft started transitioning towards becoming the core of Boeing's narrowbody lineup, whereas it was the 727 that dominated sales in the past. This shift only became more apparent as Boeing was able to further iterate on the design by releasing an even larger variant as well as a smaller version that could directly succeed the 737-200.

This aviation marvel that combines power, efficiency, and record-breaking seating in one sleek package.

While the 737-300 was stretched by about three seat rows over the 737-200, the 757 could seat an additional 60 or 70 passengers compared to the 737-300. This left a major hole in Boeing's narrowbody lineup, especially as the MD-80 was larger than the 737-300. With strong demand from airlines for a larger 737, Boeing stretched the airframe by about three to four rows, creating the 737-400. Debuting in 1988 with Piedmont Airlines, the 737-400 also proved to be an attractive aircraft like its older sibling.

What makes a stretch aircraft like the 737-400 attractive is its low per-seat costs, since the plane burns almost the same fuel as the 737-300 while offering significantly more seats. However, not all airlines need an aircraft that can seat as many as 188 passengers, and even the 737-300 was larger than the first-generation 737. As such, Boeing shrank the fuselage to create the 737-500, which entered service in 1990 to directly succeed the 737-200. Not only was the aircraft smaller, but it had the best range and takeoff performance of any 737 variant.

On its own, the 737-300's sales might not look all that impressive, as Boeing sold 1,113 examples. However, the 737-300 transformed the 737 into a family, with the 737-400 and 737-500 variants each offering something different to appeal to a wider range of customers. The 737-400 received 486 orders, while the 737-500 received 389 orders, bringing the 737 Classic up to 1,988 sales in total. In contrast, the 757 (including the later 757-300) received 1,049 orders, while the competing MD-80 received 1,191 orders.

European newcomer Airbus had made a name for itself in the 1970s with its A300, a medium-sized twinjet widebody that outsold both of its rivals (the McDonnell Douglas DC-10 and Lockheed L-1011 TriStar). It also later developed the A310 as a smaller derivative with better range, to more limited success. After years of design studies, it officially launched the clean-sheet single-aisle A320 in 1984. But to truly be successful, Airbus couldn't just build a plane as good as what the Americans had made; it needed to make a better one.

The A320-100 entered service in 1988 with Air France, with the improved A320-200 following shortly afterwards (this became the production standard). The aircraft was larger than the 737-400, which entered service the same year, and it could fly farther than any 737 variant while also cruising faster. In addition, the A320 was a significant step forward in terms of technology, introducing innovations such as a full glass cockpit, digital fly-by-wire control systems, and sidestick controls.

The 737 Classic was an appealing aircraft to 737 operators and those loyal to American manufacturers, but Airbus was ruthless with its A320. It notably sold the aircraft to Northwest Airlines, followed by United Airlines, which was widely expected to order the 737-400 at the time. The loss of the United order in particular prompted Boeing to revisit the 737 to better match the A320. What followed next was the most competitive 737 generation that Boeing had turned out yet.

The aircraft will be replaced by other highly capable, but smaller, widebodies.

The 737NG was the most significant overhaul to the 737 line yet. Boeing slightly stretched the aircraft to create the 737-700, while also upgrading the engines to the CFM56-7. In addition, the wing was fully redesigned for the 737NG, while the interior and flight deck were significantly modernized. The 737-700, positioned as a successor to the 737-300, proved to be a valiant competitor against the similarly sized Airbus A319-100. However, it was the 737-800, which succeeded the 737-400, that became the superstar of the family.

The 737-800 would compete directly against the A320-200, and it actually held some advantages. The aircraft was lighter than the A320-200, yet it could carry more passengers, and the 737-800 also had transcontinental range. The 737-800 ended up slightly outselling the A320-200, despite debuting about a decade later. Boeing would also stretch the aircraft to create the 737-900 and the later 737-900ER, but both of these proved ineffective at competing against the A321-200.

Boeing reengined and lightly improved the 737-800 and 737-900ER to create the 737 MAX 8 and 737 MAX 9, while the other MAX variants have more differences from prior models. However, these variants are very similar to their predecessors. The 737NG, however, was a much more thorough improvement compared to its predecessor, necessitated by the arrival of a competitor that was designed to render the 737 Classic obsolete.

Online shopping portals can help you save money on everyday purchases, and some let you earn rewards on top of the points or cash back you already get from your credit card.

Capital One Shopping takes a slightly different approach: It combines shopping rewards with coupon codes, price comparisons and deal alerts.

You don't need a Capital One credit card or bank account to use Capital One Shopping. The free service is available through its website, browser extension and mobile app, and you can redeem eligible Shopping Rewards for gift cards rather than cash or travel points.

Here's how Capital One Shopping works, how to earn and redeem rewards, and what to know before using it.

Capital One Shopping is a free online shopping tool that helps you find lower prices, apply available coupon codes and earn rewards at participating retailers. It can also compare prices across merchants and alert you to potential savings.

Unlike a traditional airline or hotel shopping portal, Capital One Shopping doesn't directly award airline miles or hotel points. Instead, eligible purchases and promotions earn Capital One Shopping Rewards, which can be redeemed for select retailers' electronic gift cards.

Capital One Shopping is separate from Capital One Offers, which provides shopping promotions to eligible Capital One cardholders.

You can shop through the Capital One Shopping website, install its browser extension or use its mobile app.

Keep in mind that rewards aren't necessarily credited immediately. Capital One Shopping says eligible rewards typically post within 30 to 90 days after making a qualifying purchase or completing a promotion's requirements, although some may take longer.

Returns, cancellations and other exclusions can affect reward eligibility. Using another shopping portal or applying an unlisted coupon code may also interfere with tracking, so be sure to review the offer's terms before completing your purchase.

The Capital One Shopping browser extension can help you find savings without having to manually check the portal before every purchase.

Once you've installed the extension and signed in, you can shop at participating websites as usual. Depending on the retailer, the extension can alert you to available Shopping Rewards, search for coupon codes and compare prices.

I keep the Capital One Shopping extension installed on my browser, so whenever I'm shopping online, I can easily see if any rewards are available. Even if an offer isn't always available, it's nice to know when I might be able to earn something extra on a purchase I was already planning to make.

Before checking out, review the terms of any available offers, including minimum spending requirements and product exclusions. It's also worth comparing the final price after discounts and shipping rather than choosing a retailer based solely on its advertised rewards rate.

Capital One Shopping also offers price-drop notifications for eligible products, which can be useful if you're planning a larger purchase and have some flexibility about when to buy.

Unlike traditional cash-back shopping portals such as Rakuten and TopCashback, Capital One Shopping lets you redeem your rewards for electronic gift cards at participating retailers rather than cash.

When you're ready to redeem your rewards, sign in to your Capital One Shopping account and navigate to the "My Rewards & Savings" section. From there, select "Redeem Now" to browse available gift cards and complete your redemption.

Available retailers and redemption amounts may vary, so check your account for current options.

Keep in mind that Shopping Rewards aren't the same as cash. If you'd prefer rewards that can be deposited into your bank account, a traditional cash-back shopping portal may be a better fit.

You don't need a Capital One credit card to use Capital One Shopping, so consider using a card that earns a strong rewards rate on everyday purchases or online shopping.

You can generally earn credit card rewards on eligible purchases in addition to Capital One Shopping Rewards, allowing you to earn rewards through both programs on the same purchase.

Personally, I like using my Capital One Venture X Rewards Credit Card for online purchases. While it doesn't offer bonus rewards specifically for online shopping, it earns at least 2 miles per dollar on purchases, so I know I'm getting a solid return on my spending.

For example, if I make a $200 purchase through Capital One Shopping using my Venture X, I'll earn 400 Capital One miles in addition to any eligible Shopping Rewards.

If you regularly shop online and don't mind redeeming rewards for gift cards rather than cash or travel points, Capital One Shopping is worth trying out. Just be sure to compare prices and review offer terms before making a purchase.

SOON traders, look beyond positive funding before betting on a recovery

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Soon [SOON] started the day on a bearish note, with the asset declining 14%, according to CoinGlass. However, market activity shows that the current price level has not changed the fundamental factors hinting at a possible bullish base still being in place.

The classic explanation for this is that while SOON’s short-term outlook remains bearish, its long-term prospects still show strong upside potential.

The broader market shock has played a key role in pulling capital out of the asset. Press-time data shows that SOON has recorded significant outflows in both the perpetual and spot markets.

In the perpetual market, the netflow data shows heavy selling activity. CoinGlass shows that capital outflows reached roughly $10.44 million, compared to inflows of $8.62 million, putting its net inflow at -$1.82 million.

A negative net inflow in the perpetual market often suggests that traders are pulling capital out of the market, reflecting heightened volatility.

In the spot market, the outlook is similar, with spot net inflows turning positive. In this case, a positive net inflow suggests that more SOON is being moved into exchanges than out of them, potentially indicating that traders are preparing to sell rather than accumulate.

Press-time data shows a spot net inflow of $697,000, as more capital moved into exchanges while traders prepared to sell off the asset, possibly in reaction to bearish market conditions.

The current capital movements across the perpetual and spot markets appear to reflect short-term market activity rather than a long-term shift.

The liquidation heatmap, on the other hand, points to a potentially bullish outlook for SOON over the near to long term. CoinGlass’s 1-month liquidation heatmap shows that liquidation clusters are heavily concentrated above the current price, with barely any visible clusters below it.

Price tends to move towards liquidation clusters on the chart, and right now, their heavy concentration above the current level could pull the asset towards those levels.

Importantly, the limited clusters below the price indicate that sellers could run into exhaustion. If demand grows, SOON could see a rebound, potentially creating new local highs.

For now, momentum is against long traders in the market, with $67,770 in losses recorded by long traders compared to just $4,440 in losses for short traders over the past 24 hours.

Traders in the perpetual market remain bullish across SOON contracts, based on funding rate data.

The funding rate measures whether long or short positions dominate an asset’s perpetual contracts. Presently, long positions dominate the market, as the funding rate remains positive at approximately 0.0051%, according to CoinGlass.

Bullish positioning at a time when prices are weak and the market is declining suggests that a potential rebound could still play out in the near term. Overall, capital in the perpetual market, as reflected in the funding rate data, still sits at $32.32 million at the time of writing.

This Brand-New Illinois Farmhouse Was Designed to Look Hundreds of Years Old

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The team at Buccellato Design and Architecture focused on incorporating heritage details and timeworn furnishings.

For Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, the best compliment they could receive about a project isn’t, “Wow, it looks so different in here.” It’s that a home looks like they haven’t changed a thing.

On a country road in Momence, Illinois, a beloved farmhouse stands as a timeworn landmark for the surrounding agricultural community. For the homeowners—a retired couple returning to their Midwestern roots—the property was as much a repository of memories as it was a place to create new ones. “Having grown up nearby, the property evoked a strong sense of memory and place,” says Aimee, who founded Buccellato Design and Architecture alongside her husband, Kevin. “At the outset of the project, and throughout, our clients wanted the new house to feel like it had always been there, so much so that perhaps the neighbors could be led to believe that the original house remained intact, yet amended by skillful renovation and discreet additions.”

It was an architectural sleight of hand rooted in a distinctly Midwestern philosophy: “Be good to the neighbors.” Alongside the Buccellatos, the couple approached the 6,000-square-foot rebuild with the restraint of preservationists and the imagination of storytellers. “Great care was taken to instill the home and outbuildings with a sense of history and rootedness, creating a home that feels as though it’s been part of this bucolic setting for centuries,” says Kevin of the project, which also included the restoration of the property’s original 19th-century barn and corn crib. “We drew inspiration from traditional farm complexes, where homesteads were added slowly over time, reconnecting the site with its Folk-Victorian-Farm-House and agricultural building roots.”

Inside, the narrative continues through antique white oak floors, salvaged beams, heritage florals, and rooms that borrow their palettes from the landscape. In the kitchen, a wall of windows replaces upper cabinetry, framing sunrise over the barn and walnut trees. The glass porch offers sweeping views of the Kankakee River, while a tucked-away speakeasy recalls the original farmhouse’s stone foundation.

For Kevin and Aimee, the guiding principle was a new construction home that felt “sensitive, not wasteful, polite, not ostentatious,” an exercise in knowing when architecture and interiors should defer to their surroundings. The result is a house with an invented past and a very real sense of belonging that does the Midwest—and the neighbors—proud.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Buccellato Design and ArchitectureLocation: Momence, IllinoisThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-and-a-half-bathroom home across 6,000 square feet

The entryway offers a fitting introduction to the home'’s carefully constructed sense of history, replete with nickel gap paneling, fumed and waxed antique white oak floors, and a light-filled staircase. Even the furnishings—including a vintage Persian rug and antique Jacobean gateleg table—suggest a collection acquired over generations.

Benjamin Moore’s Nelson Blue sets a serene backdrop for the sitting room, where traditional silhouettes and understated patterns make an easy pairing. “This room was designed to honor the history of this property and embody the love that our clients hold for this special place,” adds Aimee.

A tufted mohair sofa, realized in a rich umber, lends a sense of history, while an iconic feather lamp by Bunny Williams offers a reminder that even the most classic rooms benefit from a little irreverence.

In the kitchen, the Buccellatos traded upper cabinets for an expansive wall of windows overlooking the farmland, relocating the practical necessities to an adjacent dish room and larder. Salvaged white oak beams and barnboards overhead reinforce the home’s agricultural roots, while furniture-like cabinetry and leathered black quartzite countertops strike a timeless note.

With natural light pouring in from three sides, the dining room is what the Buccellatos describe as “peaceful, soft, and ethereal,” equally suited to both morning coffee and candlelit dinners. A dreamy mural wallpaper by Susan Harter references the property’s location along the Kankakee River, while formal seating lends a sense of occasion without sacrificing the home’s easygoing country charm.

Nestled among mature trees, the primary bedroom feels almost suspended in the surrounding landscape. The Buccellatos leaned into that sense of serenity with the palette. “It’s a dreamlike mix of earthy neutrals and pale blues that paints a serene backdrop for elegant details, vintage furnishings, and a delicate mix of subtle pattern,” adds Kevin.

A French country canopy bed, dressed in tailored Schumacher linen, takes center stage, while vintage-inspired furnishings and subtle pattern keep the room from feeling overly precious. An adjacent sitting room offers a place to read the morning paper or unwind with a cup of tea in the evenings.

In the vestibule, a little pattern goes a long way. A climbing floral from Schumacher in inky charcoal makes a charming counterpoint to the bedroom’s hushed palette, while a checked window bench is the ideal spot to read, giving the passageway purpose.

Pale blue walls and creamy millwork lend the primary bathroom an old-fashioned prettiness. A custom vanity introduces a tailored architectural element, while a Cowtan & Tout floral Roman shade softens the room’s more utilitarian details.

Designed as a playful respite for guests and future grandchildren, this bedroom plays off pattern and primary hues. “The red painted woodwork feels unexpected and cheerful against the blue-and-white wallpaper, striped rug, and patterned bedding,” says Aimee.

“The house and barn are nestled within nature and encircled by old-growth trees that offer a sense of place and belonging,” says Kevin of the home’s idyllic backdrop.

Founded by Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, Buccellato Design & Architecture is a practice marked by twenty-plus years of close collaboration, in architectural practice and in life. With projects on both coasts and throughout the country, the firm’s award-winning work reveals a dedication to craft, artistry, and detail. Achieved in partnership with clients, their projects reflect a purposeful sense of rootedness in the landscape and abiding devotion to beauty and the comfort of home.

Ancient star collisions could explain mysterious Little Red Dots and 'impossible' black holes

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"Little Red Dots are a hot topic in astronomy. You can compare studies to the tale of blind people who are trying to describe an elephant by touching it, and each person describes it differently based on what part they're touching."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

New research suggests that collisions between stars in the early universe were common, which could help explain the rapid growth of supermassive black holes and the existence of strange Little Red Dots seen by the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST).

The team behind this research performed simulations of the early universe and found stellar systems were densely packed, leading to stars frequently slamming into each other. While rapid collisions would have destroyed the stars involved, slower collisions would have led to repeated mergers, creating single over-massive stars, which could collapse and form larger-than-usual black holes at the end of their lives. These could be the seeds of the first supermassive black holes.

Dense stellar environments do still exist in the modern universe at the hearts of galaxies; however, these aren't conducive to the types of collisions that would birth ultra-massive stars. That is because there are already supermassive black holes at the hearts of modern galaxies, and their immense gravitational influence speeds up orbiting stars, meaning collisions that do occur are high-speed and destructive, thus failing to birth overmassive stars.

"Perhaps the strangest outcome of our simulations is that stars can merge repeatedly within the cluster until they form one gigantic star with a mass equal to that of an entire cluster," team member Claire Williams, of the University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA), said in a statement. "When a star this huge reaches the end of its life, models suggest that it may have no option but to collapse into itself and form a massive black hole."

The existence of supermassive black holes before the universe was even 1 billion years old is something that has puzzled astronomers since the JWST started spotting them in 2022.

This is because black holes should need at least 1 billion years to complete the feeding and merger processes that result in a "supermassive" status of possessing millions or billions of times the mass of the sun. One suggestion put forward by scientists to explain this is that supermassive black holes don't start out from scratch. Instead, they form from black hole seeds called intermediate-mass black holes.

Normally, when a massive star collapses, it births a stellar-mass black hole possessing a mass between 10 and 100 times the mass of the sun. This is considerably smaller than supermassive black holes that dominate the hearts of all modern galaxies with masses millions to billions of times the mass of our star.

Intermediate-mass black holes are thought to sit between these two families of cosmic titans with masses between 100 and 100,000 times that of the sun. The problem is, no single star should have enough mass to collapse and create an intermediate-mass black hole. But that is valid only for the conditions we see in the "modern" 13.8-billion-year-old universe in our immediate cosmic vicinity. Conditions were very different further out into the universe and further back in time.

Indeed, such seeds emerged from these new simulations in environments of dense star clustering, which could solve this mystery by allowing those growth processes to get a head start.

"Dense star clusters provide a natural birthplace for massive black-hole seeds in our model," Williams said. "The same environment that forms the black hole can also provide stars and gas that influence its subsequent growth."

However, this could be a two-for-one deal. The JWST has also been routinely discovering astronomical bodies called Little Red Dots in the early universe. Little Red Dots are curious because they seem to have completely disappeared by the time the cosmos reached 2 billion years old.

Little Red Dots are extremely compact objects in the early universe that get their moniker from the fact that they look like red dots.

"Little Red Dots are a hot topic in astronomy. You can compare studies of people trying to understand Little Red Dots to the tale of blind people who are trying to describe an elephant by touching it, and each person describes it differently based on what part they’re touching," team member and UCLA researcher Smadar Naoz said. "Our research connects several of those perspectives together and may help us see more of the elephant."

There have been many suggestions about the true nature of these objects, but one theory suggests that they possess a rapidly growing black hole at their hearts.

The team's simulations suggest that Little Red Dots may be dense star clusters that are constantly being rocked by repeated stellar collisions. This results in a very large star, which eventually collapses to become an intermediate-mass black hole.But if that is the case, why were there no Little Red Dots around to celebrate the 2 billionth birthday of the universe?

Less than 1 billion years after the Big Bang, the universe was devoid of well-ordered spiral galaxies, but was instead filled with dense and chaotic clusters of star birth. As these regions were rocked with frequent star collisions, the conditions were right for ultra-massive stars to form and collapse into intermediate-mass black holes.

However, as the universe cooled and expanded, these sites became the building blocks of far less chaotic galaxies. Thus, if Little Red Dots are chaotic star clusters needed to form intermediate-mass black holes, progress toward an equilibrium state could explain why they suddenly disappeared from the universe. The cosmos just wasn't extreme enough to maintain Little Red Dots anymore.The team's research was published on Monday (Oct. 26) in the Astrophysical Journal.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

Southwest Companion Pass — 2-for-1 Flights for 2 Years

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The ultimate domestic travel rewards benefit for families and couples

Imagine this: you book a flight for yourself, and your spouse — or your best friend, or your mom — flies with you for the cost of taxes alone. Not a discount. Not a sale. Completely. Free. For up to two full calendar years. That's the Southwest Companion Pass, and it is, without question, the single most powerful perk in all of domestic travel rewards. The ChooseFI community has a saying: if you only ever pursue one travel rewards strategy in your life, make it this one. Here's the math that makes your jaw drop. A round-trip Southwest flight from Denver to Cancún might run $400 per person. A family of four flying during spring break? You're looking at $1,600 cash. But with the Companion Pass, you pay for two tickets and the other two fly free — cutting your out-of-pocket cost in half, every single flight, for nearly two years. Run the numbers over a year of typical family travel and you're looking at **$2,000 to $8,000 in total value** from a single benefit. The CPP (cents per point) range here — **1.5 to 5.9 cents per point** — is what makes this genuinely remarkable. Southwest's Rapid Rewards program values points at roughly 1.4 cents toward any flight. But the Companion Pass doesn't just redeem points efficiently; it *doubles the value of every redemption you make while it's active*. That's the pricing inefficiency the loyalty system has quietly built in, and the FI community is uniquely positioned to exploit it. Think about what 135,000 points normally buys: roughly $1,890 worth of Southwest flights. Solid, but not earth-shattering. Now attach the Companion Pass, and those same points — redeemed strategically over two years — effectively unlock $3,780 or more in flight value for your household. No blackout dates. No seat restrictions. No change fees. Just book, fly, repeat. This isn't a first-class lounge or a lie-flat bed over the Pacific. It's something arguably more valuable for the FI-minded family: **sustainable, repeatable, nearly free domestic travel** that works with your actual life.

The Companion Pass isn't a points redemption — it's a status benefit. That distinction is everything, and it's the insight that separates people who get this from people who miss it entirely.

Here's how it works: Southwest grants the Companion Pass when you earn 135,000 qualifying Rapid Rewards points in a single calendar year. Once you hit that threshold, you designate one person as your companion, and that person flies with you on every flight — paid or points — for the remainder of that calendar year plus the entire following calendar year.

The key word is "earn." Points transferred in from partners count. Credit card signup bonuses count. Shopping portal purchases count. Almost every way you accumulate points counts toward the 135,000 threshold — except points purchased directly from Southwest.

The pricing inefficiency here is a calendar arbitrage opportunity. If you earn the Companion Pass on January 2nd, you get it for the rest of that year and all of the next year — nearly two full calendar years of 2-for-1 flights. If you earn it on November 15th, you get roughly 13 months. The strategy is to time your credit card signup bonuses to post in January, maximizing your benefit window.

Most ChooseFI members build their 135,000 points through a combination of strategies:

Chase Ultimate Rewards → Southwest Rapid Rewards (1:1, Instant) Chase transfers to Southwest at a 1:1 ratio and the transfer is instant — points appear in your Rapid Rewards account within minutes. This makes Chase points (earned on cards like the Chase Sapphire Preferred or Ink Business cards) the most efficient fuel for this strategy.

Marriott Bonvoy → Southwest Rapid Rewards (3:1, 1-5 days) Marriott transfers at a 3:1 ratio (3 Marriott points = 1 Southwest point), and transfers take 1-5 days. This is a less efficient path — use it only if you have a surplus of Marriott points and no better option.

The most reliable path to 135,000 points is stacking Southwest credit card signup bonuses. Southwest's consumer and business cards frequently offer bonuses of 50,000-75,000 points each. A couple who each opens one card, or one person who opens both a personal and business card, can often hit the threshold with signup bonuses alone — before spending a dollar on the portal.

Since Southwest charges no fuel surcharges on award flights and points never expire, there's no penalty for building this balance over several months. The Rapid Rewards program is revenue-based with dynamic pricing, meaning points cover the full fare including taxes — no hidden redemption fees to erode your value.

Free calculator shows when you could reach financial independence — plus a savings rate tracker, expense audit, and more.

Log into your Chase Ultimate Rewards account and your Southwest Rapid Rewards account and tally your existing points. You need 135,000 qualifying Rapid Rewards points earned in a single calendar year to trigger the Companion Pass. Note: points already sitting in your Rapid Rewards account from prior years don't count — you need to earn 135,000 in the target calendar year.

The optimal window is October through December of the prior year. Apply for Southwest credit cards (personal and/or business) so that your signup bonuses post to your Rapid Rewards account in January. Verify with Southwest's current card offers — signup bonuses of 50,000-75,000 points are common. A couple can each apply for a card; a solo pursuer can apply for both a personal and a business card if they have qualifying self-employment income.

Call Chase or Southwest after your first statement closes to confirm when points will post. Timing is everything.

Once your signup bonuses post, calculate how many points you still need. If you're short:

Southwest will notify you by email once you've crossed 135,000 qualifying points. Log into your Rapid Rewards account at southwest.com and navigate to "My Account" → "Companion Pass" to confirm the benefit is active.

You can designate — and change — your companion up to three times per calendar year. Name your most frequent travel partner first. Go to southwest.com, log in, and find the Companion Pass section to add their name and contact information.

Searching is simple: go to southwest.com and search any route. Toggle between "$ Dollars" and "Rapid Rewards Points" to see your cost. When you book with points and your companion is designated, Southwest will automatically prompt you to add your companion to the same flight. Your companion pays taxes only — typically $5.60 each way for domestic flights.

Pro tip: Southwest opens its booking window 330 days in advance. For peak travel like spring break or holiday weeks, set a calendar reminder and book the day the window opens.

The Companion Pass works because Southwest treats it as a status benefit rather than a points redemption. Most airline companion programs require you to burn miles for the second ticket or limit usage to specific fare classes. Southwest flips this model: once you earn the pass, your companion flies on every single flight you take — paid or award — for the remainder of the qualifying year plus the entire following calendar year. The only cost is taxes. Because Southwest has no blackout dates, no fuel surcharges, and no change fees, the pass amplifies in value with every trip you take. The January-earning calendar arbitrage is the key optimization that turns a good benefit into an extraordinary one.

Let's make this real. It's March, and your family of four wants to spend a week in Orlando this July — theme parks, pool time, the works.

A quick search on southwest.com shows round-trip flights from Chicago Midway to Orlando for $320 per person. For four tickets, that's $1,280 cash. Standard family travel math: painful but doable.

You have the Companion Pass active, earned back in January. You book two tickets: yours at 22,000 Rapid Rewards points (worth ~$308 at 1.4 cents per point), and your spouse's ticket at $5.60 in taxes as your designated companion. Your two kids? You use 44,000 more points for their tickets. Total out-of-pocket: 66,000 points + $11.20 in taxes.

Let's value those 66,000 points at their Chase transfer cost — you transferred them 1:1 from Chase Ultimate Rewards, where they were worth roughly $825 in travel via the portal. But by pairing them with the Companion Pass, your spouse flies free, effectively doubling your points' purchasing power on this trip.

Cash price: $1,280. Your cost: 66,000 points + $11.20. That's a CPP of roughly 1.9 cents per point on the base redemption — but accounting for your companion flying free, the effective CPP on the entire trip rockets to 3.8 cents per point. Well within the 1.5–5.9 cent range where this sweet spot lives.

And here's what makes Southwest specifically wonderful for families: no change fees. Book in March, change in June when your kid's baseball tournament schedule shifts, rebook without penalty. No one in the FI community is paying $200-per-person change fees.

The experience itself? Economy class on Southwest, open seating, snacks, and friendly crew. It's not lie-flat business class to Tokyo — but that's not the point. The point is your family of four flies to Florida for a week, and the flights barely register on your annual budget. That's FI-level travel.

Time your credit card applications so signup bonuses post in January of your target year. A bonus that posts January 5th gives you until December 31st of the following year to use the Companion Pass — nearly 24 months of 2-for-1 flights. This single timing decision can mean the difference between 13 months and 23 months of value.

The Rapid Rewards Shopping Portal (rapidrewardsshopping.com) awards points on purchases at hundreds of retailers. Even after you've earned the Companion Pass, continuing to earn Rapid Rewards points means more flights at lower point costs. Treat it like a cashback portal and check it before any online purchase.

Southwest opens availability 330 days in advance. For Thanksgiving, Christmas, spring break, and summer weekends in popular markets, set a calendar reminder and book the exact morning the window opens. Southwest doesn't have blackout dates, but high-demand flights sell out in Wanna Get Away fare class — the cheapest points tier — quickly.

You can change your designated companion up to three times per calendar year. If your life situation changes — a different travel partner, a new relationship, a child aging into a separate ticket — use this flexibility. Just don't burn all three changes frivolously.

Purchased points do not count toward the 135,000 qualifying threshold for the Companion Pass. Don't make this mistake when you're close to the threshold — transfer from Chase instead.

Some people try to earn the Companion Pass in November or December. You'll get it for only a few weeks before the calendar resets. The math simply doesn't work. January earning is the only strategy worth pursuing.

The Southwest Rapid Rewards Performance Business Credit Card (or similar business cards) have separate signup bonuses that stack with personal card bonuses. If you have any self-employment income — freelancing, a side hustle, a rental property — you likely qualify. This is often the fastest path to 135,000 points for a single applicant.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

The Southwest Companion Pass was practically invented for the FI-minded family. If you have a consistent travel partner — a spouse, a partner, a parent you visit annually — and you fly domestically more than two or three times a year, this benefit will transform your travel budget.

Minimum points to get started: You need to earn 135,000 qualifying Rapid Rewards points in a single calendar year. In practice, this means you either need access to Southwest credit card signup bonuses (which requires good to excellent credit, typically 700+) or a substantial existing balance of Chase Ultimate Rewards points to transfer at 1:1.

How it fits the FI strategy: The Companion Pass is a force multiplier. Every dollar you would have spent on a second ticket stays in your investment accounts instead. Over a two-year Companion Pass window, a couple who takes six domestic trips per year could easily bank $2,000-$4,000 in travel savings — real money that compounds.

Who this is NOT for: Solo travelers get zero benefit from the Companion Pass. International travelers who primarily fly non-Southwest routes won't find much use here. And if you're averse to economy class or require a specific airline's premium cabin, this redemption isn't your path.

The Southwest Companion Pass earns a **moderate difficulty rating** — and that rating is almost entirely about the *earning*, not the booking. Once you have the Companion Pass, booking is literally the simplest award experience in travel rewards: search southwest.com, find your flight, click "Add Companion." There are no partner airlines to navigate, no availability calendars to decode, no call centers to endure. It's a 5/5 on ease of booking for a reason. The moderate rating comes from **three earning challenges**: **1. The credit requirement.** Southwest credit cards require good to excellent credit. If you're early in your credit-building journey, this strategy may need to wait 12-18 months while you strengthen your profile. **2. The timing discipline.** Earning the pass is straightforward, but earning it in January takes planning. You need to apply for cards 2-3 months in advance and coordinate signup bonus posting dates — something most people have never had to think about. **3. The 135,000-point threshold.** This is a real number. It requires intentional credit card strategy, not casual spending. For a single applicant without a business card, it may require supplementing with Chase transfers or portal activity. **How to overcome these challenges:** - Start planning in **October or November** for a January earn target - Check whether you qualify for a Southwest business card — it dramatically simplifies reaching the threshold - Use the Rapid Rewards Shopping Portal year-round to passively accumulate qualifying points If you've successfully applied for a travel rewards credit card before and you have a consistent travel partner, you can absolutely do this. The ChooseFI community has helped hundreds of families earn the Companion Pass — and not one of them regretted the effort.

Open a personal and/or business Southwest credit card so that signup bonuses of 50,000 to 75,000 points each will post to your Rapid Rewards account in January. Call to confirm when points will post after your first statement closes.

Once signup bonuses post, calculate your gap. Transfer Chase Ultimate Rewards at 1:1 (instant) to fill any shortfall. Use the Rapid Rewards Shopping Portal for additional earning on everyday purchases.

After Southwest confirms your Companion Pass, log in to southwest.com and designate your travel partner. Book flights as normal — Southwest will prompt you to add your companion for taxes only ($5.60 each way domestic).

Set calendar reminders for Thanksgiving, Christmas, spring break, and summer travel. Southwest opens its booking window 330 days in advance. Book the morning the window opens to lock in Wanna Get Away fares at the lowest point costs.

The Companion Pass is valid for the remainder of the calendar year in which you earn it plus the entire following calendar year. If you earn it on January 2nd, you get nearly 24 months of 2-for-1 flights. If you earn it in November, you get roughly 14 months. This is why timing your earning to January is the critical optimization.

Yes. Signup bonuses, spending on Southwest cards, points transferred from Chase Ultimate Rewards, and points earned through the Rapid Rewards Shopping Portal all count as qualifying points. The only points that do not count are those purchased directly from Southwest.

The Southwest Companion Pass is the single most powerful domestic travel rewards benefit available to FI-minded families and couples. At a cost of 135,000 qualifying points — achievable through credit card signup bonuses and Chase transfers — you unlock nearly two full years of 2-for-1 flights on every Southwest route with no blackout dates and no change fees. A family of four taking six domestic trips per year can realistically save $2,000 to $4,000 annually, money that goes straight back into your investment accounts. These 10 New Features Are Coming to iOS 27.3

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Apple dropped the first developer beta for iOS 27.3 on Tuesday. While this update isn't as large as iOS 27 proper, it's surprisingly feature-filled for a 0.3 update. This is only a beta, so anything could (and likely will) change before Apple officially launches the update. But, as it stands, iOS 27.3 tests 10 new features—including one feature the company trialed in the previous beta.

Technically, anyone with a compatible iPhone can try out iOS 27.3 right now—assuming you enroll your Apple Account in the developer program. But a word of warning: Betas can be temperamental, and could be risky. As such, I'd recommend avoiding the beta until Apple releases the public beta, which comes after a bit more testing to iron out larger issues. Either way, make sure to make a full backup of your iPhone to a Mac or PC first, as issues with the beta could result in permanent data loss.

With watchOS 27, Apple released "Audio Intelligence," a new set of features for Apple Watch Series 12 and Ultra 4. Audio Intelligence uses the watches' new S11 chip, along with on-device AI and cloud-based AI, to run new features, like Sound Recognition and Shazam (music recognition).

With iOS 27.3, however, the company is now officially testing the other two Audio Intelligence features it originally announced with watchOS 27. First, there's Live Rewind: By double-clicking the watch crown, you can pull up a transcript of the last 15 seconds of a conversation. Siri Recap, on the other hand, displays summaries of conversations you had that day, in case you need a refresher on any chats you might have otherwise forgotten about,

The company generated some controversy when it first announced these two Audio Intelligence features, since, on the surface, it sounds like these new Apple Watches are essentially spying on people all day long. According to Apple, however, that isn't really the case. The company says Audio Intelligence is private, and never records or stores any audio. No speakers are ever identified, and text results (both transcripts and summaries) are deleted automatically after a set period of time unless the user saves them to the Siri app. You can read more about how Audio Intelligence works here.

Despite their claims, I'm sure there will still be strong feelings surrounding these Audio Intelligence features. We'll have to see whether Apple can prove Live Rewind and Siri Recap aren't all that creepy throughout the iOS 27.3 beta process.

iOS 27.3 also introduces app actions for Siri AI. Apple is testing new abilities for Siri AI, including organizing files, planning events, or adding photos to albums. You could ask Siri to organize your Downloads folder, or delete files that haven't been opened in more than a year. You could ask Siri to find a coffee shop you went to last week, or to save an article to your Reading List in Safari. You could ask Siri to check your calendar and set up a time for an event based on when you're free. You can even ask the assistant to schedule, edit, or unsend messages.

For example, you could ask Siri AI to help you plan a trip with your friends. You could ask the AI to put together a themed playlist in Apple Music, schedule a message to a group about an upcoming trip, organize the websites you've been visiting in Safari into a tab group, and, after the trip, ask Siri to move your best photos to your favorites album. These new actions work in the following apps: Calendar, Files, Finder, Journal, Mail, Maps, Messages, Music, Notes, Photos, Preview, Reminders, Safari, Shortcuts, and Weather. Developers can also add Siri AI app actions support to their own apps.

Apple started testing its new Health app with the iOS 27.2 beta, but pulled the redesign before the official release. Now, with iOS 27.3, the new app is back. The new Health app has a health dashboard for the first time, as well as new features like Health Age and movement assessments. It seems to be pulling from some popular competitor services, hopefully, for the better. For more on the new Health app, check out Lifehacker health editor Beth Skwarecki's explainer.

Continuing on the Health track, iOS 27.3 also introduces "Gym Strength." This is a new Fitness+ feature that offers a personalized four-week strength-training plan. You can customize these plans to meet your goals, experience level, and schedule, and they adapt as you progress. You'll get recommendations based on your experience with exercises, reps, and sets, including video demos for exercises. You can access Gym Strength on both iPhone and iPad, and Apple Watches are optional—though you can log your progress with the wearable if you have it.

Vertical tabs have been a favorite among browser aficionados, as they give you more screen real estate in your browser while keeping your tabs off the side. Apple hasn't made the jump—until now. This feature works with iPads running iPadOS 27.3 as well as Macs running macOS 27.3.

Another new Safari feature in testing is "Adaptive Dark Mode." This feature forces websites into a dark theme, if they don't have a native theme of their own. There are third-party apps that can do this, but this is the first time Apple has offered this feature built into Safari.

Apple is also testing new StandBy experiences with iOS 27.3, which were previously only available on the iPhone Duo. When you turn your iPhone sideways while plugged in, you'll find new Analog and Digital Clock faces with new customization options. There's also a new Photos face, with the option to pull images from Shared Albums.

"Reachability" pulls your iPhone's display down towards the bottom of the screen, so you can access items towards the top of the display with one hand. It's a useful feature for those of us with big iPhones, and now, there's no iPhone bigger than the Duo. With iOS 27.3, Apple is testing Reachability for its first foldable phone. It's a bit like the inverse of how Reachability currently works: You can swipe on the left or right side of your Duo's display to shift the other side of the screen towards your current position. If you're holding your Duo in your left hand, a swipe of the left side will pull the right side over to the left, and vice versa.

Apple is also rolling out new languages for Siri AI in iOS 27.3. Now, Apple's smart assistant supports Traditional Chinese, Vietnamese, Turkish, and Norwegian.

If you have an Apple Intelligence-compatible iPad running iPadOS 27.3, you now have access to Siri Mode in Camera. As with iOS, you can open the Camera app on iPadOS to access Siri Mode, and ask Siri about your surroundings.

Safe Word: The Best Jewelry Launches of November 2026

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Safe Word is a series highlighting the latest trends in the jewelry market, keeping you up to date on all things that sparkle and shine.

November’s jewelry launches are looking both backward and forward, with designers revisiting their roots, reworking archival ideas, and stepping into entirely new territory. First up, Pomellato reaches back to 1997 to revive “Moneta Orientale” as a colorful modern talisman; David Yurman celebrates the magic of a New York holiday with Michael B. Jordan, Yerin Ha, and Inde Navarrette; Vhernier revisits its iconic “Trottola” design with new proportions and contrasting materials; Di Volpe reinterprets the classic tennis bracelet with customizable gemstones in “DiSegno”; and Brent Neale transforms the classic bow into sculptural fine jewelry with “Wrap It Up.” From archival revivals and meaningful motifs to innovative designs and new creative directions, November’s launches prove that sometimes the best way forward starts with looking back. For the rest of the month, we’ve got you covered on all the jewelry-related happenings worth knowing about.

Pomellato Revives Its 1997 Moneta Orientale Pendant as a Colorful Talisman

Pomellato is looking to its archives for its latest good-luck charm. For the newest chapter of “Memorie d’Archivio,” the maison revives Moneta Orientale, a 1997 pendant inspired by the ancient symbolism of a circle surrounding a square, representing the harmony between heaven and earth. Made in 33 stone-and-pavé combinations, the design pairs colorful hard stones including black jade, amazonite, sodalite, rhodochrosite, and labradorite with Pomellato’s signature irregular pavé of diamonds, sapphires, rubies, and more. Offered in two proportions, each pendant transforms the archival motif into a modern talisman for prosperity and protection.

Bibi van der Velden Makes Her High Jewelry Debut With ‘Eclipse’

Bibi van der Velden is entering a new era with “Eclipse,” the designer’s first high jewelry collection and most refined exploration of diamonds to date. Inspired by the fleeting moment when light gives way to darkness, the collection includes two six-piece suites: one in white diamonds and another defined by an ombré of diamonds into gray spinels deepening into black. Van der Velden’s signature alligator motif is abstracted into fluid, sculptural silhouettes, with scales engraved into the white gold on the reverse of each piece. “With Eclipse, I wanted to take those familiar elements and transform them into something more abstract and sculptural,” she says. “It’s a collection about transformation, which has always been at the heart of my work.”

Aflalo and Leandra Medine Cohen Turn Everyday Indulgences Into Fine Jewelry

Aflalo is finding the humor in our obsession with wellness with “The Vanities,” a new two-piece fine jewelry capsule designed exclusively by Leandra Medine Cohen. Playing with the contradiction between longevity and life’s little indulgences, the collection transforms two unlikely everyday accessories into precious objects. The Memento Mori is a 14-karat yellow gold cigar band set with more than four carats of diamonds, designed to slip over an Oura Ring, while The Libertine is a sterling silver pendant case adorned with diamonds and a wild-haired woman outlined in gold, designed to hold nicotine pouches or other small essentials. Equal parts extravagant and irreverent, the collection makes a playful case for enjoying life’s pleasures without sacrificing personal style.

Julia Berolzheimer’s Hart Jewelry Collection Draws on Childhood Memories

Hart is teaming up with Julia Berolzheimer for a new jewelry collection that celebrates the art of the stack. Inspired by Berolzheimer’s childhood memories of her grandmother’s California ranch and her collection of Art Deco minaudières, the collaboration brings together whimsical charms, colorful gemstones, and meaningful talismans designed to be mixed, layered, and restyled. Botanical motifs take shape in Tree of Life coins, daisies, bees, and fruit-inspired charms, while carnelian, turquoise, and pink opal add playful pops of color. One standout is the Perfume Vesta Case, a modern interpretation of a Victorian-era accessory that can hold a sample-sized fragrance and hang alongside other charms. With interchangeable chains, cords, and pendants, the collection makes a case for jewelry that feels just as personal as the memories behind it.

David Yurman Captures the Magic of a New York Holiday

David Yurman is celebrating the magic of the holidays in New York City with a new campaign featuring global brand ambassador Michael B. Jordan alongside actresses Yerin Ha and Inde Navarrette. Set against a snow-covered Washington Square Park, the campaign captures a series of quintessential New York moments, from Jordan and Ha facing off in a spirited card game to Navarrette hailing a cab in search of the perfect holiday gift. Throughout the campaign, the cast wears some of the house’s most recognizable designs, including 18-karat gold Cable bracelets and pieces from the “Starburst” and “Armory” collections. Directed by Bradford Young and photographed by Drew Vickers, the campaign brings together the spirit of the city, the joy of togetherness, and the timeless appeal of David Yurman’s jewelry.

Vhernier Reintroduces Its Iconic ‘Trottola’ Design With New Proportions

Vhernier is revisiting one of its most recognizable designs with a new interpretation of “Trottola,” the sculptural collection originally introduced in 2003. Inspired by the continuous motion of a spinning top, the latest designs play with proportions, contrasting materials, and asymmetrical shapes across rings, bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Polished rose gold meets glossy black jet, with select pieces featuring diamond pavé for an added contrast of light and dark. The collection also focuses on versatility, from rings designed for stacking to convertible earclips that can be worn as studs or dangling earrings. With its fluid forms and unexpected material combinations, “Trottola” brings a fresh perspective to Vhernier’s signature sculptural approach to jewelry.

Di Volpe is rethinking the classic tennis bracelet with “DiSegno,” a new customizable fine jewelry system designed to change with its wearer. Using concealed, patent-pending connections, individual cultured diamonds and gemstones, called Elements, can be removed, rearranged, or added by hand, allowing wearers to transform bracelets into necklaces or earrings without compromising the seamless look of traditional tennis jewelry. The collection launches with 28 stone-and-cut combinations, including rubies, emeralds, sapphires, and diamonds, alongside a limited-edition collaboration with Lebanese artist Ali Cha’aban featuring five sculptural designs. With new cuts, colors, and artist collaborations planned for future releases, “DiSegno” makes the case for fine jewelry that’s never quite finished.

Brent Neale Riffs on the Bow in Fine Jewelry

Brent Neale is putting a bow on the holiday season with “Wrap It Up,” a new fine jewelry collection that transforms the familiar ribbon motif into sculptural works of art. Drawing inspiration from Old Hollywood glamour and the femininity of the 1960s, the designer plays with the contrast between rigid precious metals and the soft movement of fabric, crafting bows that twist, curl, and unfurl in 18-karat gold and platinum. Colored gemstones sit nestled within the ribbon-like forms, while pavé diamonds accentuate every curve and fold. Equal parts playful and precious, the collection brings a little gift-wrapped glamour to the jewelry box.

Grace Winfrey’s ‘Poppy Fortuna’ Collection Reimagines Good-Luck Charms

Grace Winfrey is bringing a little extra luck to the jewelry box with “Poppy Fortuna,” a new fine jewelry collection inspired by the natural beauty of the Italian landscape and symbols of good fortune. Scarlet poppies, sunflowers, bees, four-leaf clovers, horseshoes, and ladybugs come to life through vibrant gemstones and intricate pavé, creating miniature mosaics of color and texture. Highlights include a Lucky Bangle combining three good-luck charms, sculptural sunflower earrings with tiny ladybugs tucked into their petals, and layered charm necklaces designed to feel like modern keepsakes. Handcrafted in New York City, each piece transforms familiar symbols of prosperity, protection, and happiness into playful, colorful works of art.

Robinson Pelham Channels ’90s Glamour for Its Anniversary

Robinson Pelham is closing out its 30th anniversary celebrations with “Suite 96,” a new campaign and collection launch inspired by the glamour of late-’90s London. Set inside a private suite at St. James’s Hotel & Club in Mayfair, the campaign considers jewelry the ultimate room service, with diamonds arriving on trays, gold spilling out of wardrobes, and plenty of pieces to layer before a night out. At the center is “Studio 30,” a four-piece anniversary collection featuring diamond necklaces and statement earrings, including the sculptural Via and Hira necklaces, cascading diamond drops, and the bold Solstice Supreme. Alongside the anniversary designs, the house introduces new collections and additions to existing favorites, from chunky gold links and graphic heart rings to colorful enamel and gemstone combinations. Together, the launches celebrate three decades of Robinson Pelham’s bold, playful approach to fine jewelry.

Le Vian and Jared Revive Georgian-Era Romance

Le Vian is bringing a little old-world romance to Jared Jewelers with its new exclusive “Georgian Chocolate & Nude Diamonds” collection. Inspired by the candlelit jewels of the Georgian era, when jewelry was designed to capture and reflect the warmth of natural light, the collection rethinks historic buttercup settings in polished Honey Gold, with eight rounded prongs framing each natural diamond. The designs pair Le Vian’s signature Chocolate and Nude Diamonds with warm gold detailing across solitaire rings, anniversary bands, tennis bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Select styles introduce colorful gemstones including Chocolate Quartz, Green Apple Peridot, and Grape Amethyst, giving the vintage-inspired silhouettes a contemporary update. With its sculptural settings and warm-toned stones, the collection brings the romance of antique jewelry into the present.

Wempe’s ‘Au:thentic’ Celebrates the Art of Goldsmithing

Wempe’s new gender-inclusive fine jewelry collection, “Au:thentic,” puts craftsmanship front and center, finding inspiration in the foundational skills taught to aspiring goldsmiths. The collection takes its shape from the jump ring, one of the first techniques Patrick Sieroslawski learned as an apprentice more than 22 years ago, translating its form to necklaces, bracelets, rings, earrings, and pendants. Handcrafted at Wempe’s atelier in Germany, the pieces come in 18-karat yellow, white, and rose gold, with select styles set with diamonds. “Au:thentic” also marks the beginning of a multiyear rollout, with three additional chapters set to expand on its design language through twists, wraps, and interlocking links.

Safe Word: The Best Jewelry Launches of November 2026

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Safe Word is a series highlighting the latest trends in the jewelry market, keeping you up to date on all things that sparkle and shine.

November’s jewelry launches are looking both backward and forward, with designers revisiting their roots, reworking archival ideas, and stepping into entirely new territory. First up, Pomellato reaches back to 1997 to revive “Moneta Orientale” as a colorful modern talisman; David Yurman celebrates the magic of a New York holiday with Michael B. Jordan, Yerin Ha, and Inde Navarrette; Vhernier revisits its iconic “Trottola” design with new proportions and contrasting materials; Di Volpe reimagines the classic tennis bracelet with customizable gemstones in “DiSegno”; and Brent Neale transforms the classic bow into sculptural fine jewelry with “Wrap It Up.”

Elsewhere, Grace Winfrey looks to Italian good-luck charms for the colorful “Poppy Fortuna”; Robinson Pelham checks into “Suite 96” to celebrate 30 years of bold jewelry; Le Vian brings Georgian-era romance to Jared with a new collection of warm-toned diamonds; and Wempe puts traditional goldsmithing at the center of “au:thentic,” inspired by the humble jump ring. From archival revivals and meaningful motifs to innovative designs and new creative directions, November’s launches prove that sometimes the best way forward starts with looking back.

For the rest of the month, we’ve got you covered on all the jewelry-related happenings worth knowing about.

Travel’s Next Dollar: Capital Moves November 2026

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Capital Moves, organized by month and updated daily, tracks every notable capital allocation announcement across global travel, providing a running tally of where travel's next dollar is going.

Select a category to filter the records below. Selections combine across the four groups, and every count updates to show what remains available. Choose “All” in a group to clear that group. Counts are of transactions and developments, not unique companies.

Each development has a primary Next Dollar category, reflecting its capital allocation. The counts update with your other selections.

Very Online is your best source for news about online travel, from startups to the biggest online travel agencies.

Tags: capital moves, finance, mergers and acquisitions, next dollar, private equity, venture capital

Skift provides the insights. You make the decisions.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Skift's capital tracker for early November 2026 shows travel's largest companies putting new money into two very different places: consolidation and shareholder returns. The headline transaction is Lufthansa Group's signed agreement with the Portuguese government to buy an initial 44.9% stake in TAP Air Portugal for €1.025 billion (about $1.19 billion), with the holding able to rise to 49.9% and the total to roughly €1.2 billion if employee-reserved shares are not taken up. Portugal keeps majority ownership, and closing requires regulatory approval. Everything else logged in the window is balance-sheet work rather than expansion: Marriott disclosed roughly $4 billion returned to shareholders through October 28 while expanding its revolving credit facility from $4.5 billion to $5 billion and issuing $1.25 billion in senior notes; Southwest Airlines closed a $1.25 billion two-tranche bond offering ahead of a November maturity; Ryanair authorized a new €800 million buyback to begin in February 2027 after completing a €750 million program in September; and aftermarket parts distributor AvAir closed $170 million in revolving credit facilities with a six-bank group to fund inventory and acquisitions. The pattern across the tracker is capital flowing to M&A, refinancing, and returns rather than to demand generation or distribution.
